Hybrid Path-Sums for Hybrid Quantum Programs
Christophe Chareton, Jad Issa, Mathieu Nguyen, Nicolas Blanco, Sébastien Bardin

Abstract
. As quantum computing becomes an emerging reality, designing efficient quantum programming capabilities is becoming more and more important. Particularly, the debugging and validation of quantum programs is of paramount importance, as these programs are by definition hard to test. Static analysis and formal verification methods for quantum programs started to emerge a few years now, yet they often miss hybrid quantum/classical reasoning facilities with, e.g., generic quantum control, classical control and classical computation instructions. In this paper, we lay out the foundations of a framework for the automated formal verification of (full) hybrid quantum programs featuring both classical and quantum control, measurement and hybrid data structures. In particular, we propose: (1) a novel symbolic representation for describing and manipulating sets of hybrid quantum/classical states called Hybrid Path-Sums (HPS); (2) a set of rewriting rules providing a rich mechanism for simplifying and reasoning on these symbolic hybrid states, and (3) a core assertion language to specify equivalence of hybrid quantum programs, the satisfaction of properties on (parts of) hybrid states, and the extraction of probabilistic statements about the program behavior. We prove the correctness of the novel symbolic representation, of its rewriting system and of the specification system. Finally, we propose a full implementation of this framework as a dedicated symbolic execution engine for hybrid programs. We present an evaluation of a set of representative hybrid case-studies from the literature, showcasing the advantage of our approach and its efficiency compared to state-of-the-art solutions.



1.
Introduction

Context.

While quantum acceleration may soon become an industrial reality
(
5
;
1
)
, it remains unclear how to evaluate the correctness of quantum programs. On the one hand, the only way to extract useful information from the state of a
quantum system is through
quantum measurement
which collapses the
state and irremediably modifies it, making runtime debugging and monitoring
impossible. On the other hand, as quantum computations are probabilistic by nature, end-to-end testing requires enormous numbers of
repetitions to establish confidence in the probability distribution of the
output. Therefore,
robust, expressive,
efficient, and tractable formal verification tools are absolute
requirements for the industrialization of quantum computing.

Formal verification relies on mathematically establishing the correctness of a program
for any possible input. It is already preferred over testing in critical
applications such as the formalization of mathematics
(
42
)
,
software architectures
(
57
;
60
)
, and industrial
development systems
(
8
;
28
)
. Its application for
quantum programs, on the other hand, is still in its infancy. Yet,
there are already promising results
involving symbolic representations
(
7
;
4
;
3
;
82
)
, specification language
design
(
22
)
, deductive
systems
(
92
;
91
)
and the development of
practical
tools
(
95
;
48
;
18
;
23
)

– see
(
19
)
for a survey. But none of these works
supports all the core features necessary for the automated formal verification
of
hybrid quantum programs
.

The Problem of Hybrid Programs Formalization.

Hybrid computation is ubiquitous in the literature, with algorithms intertwining
classical and quantum computation such as QAOA/VQE
(
71
;
35
;
81
)
, or embedding quantum computations
within classical
repeat until success
control patterns. Furthermore, many purely
quantum algorithms such as Quantum Phase Estimation gain in performance when
split into smaller components and
hybridized
(
84
;
53
)
, and
even seemingly purely quantum primitives end up being hybridized to support
error detection and correction
(
56
;
43
)
, which is critical in taming the intrinsic high noise levels in qubit systems.

In hybrid architectures, a quantum processor (QPU) communicates with the
classical CPU, where measurement translates quantum superpositions into classical
non-determinism. The challenge is therefore to design a formalism
flexible enough to handle primitives of both models as well as their
communication through measurement and quantum-, classical-, or hybrid- control.

Goals and Challenges.
We aim to provide the foundations of a
verification framework for
full
hybrid quantum programming, providing

measurement

(M)
,
hybrid data representation

(HD)
and
classical

(CC)

and quantum control

(QC)
. In addition, the representation should be
compact

(CT)
, avoiding representations of exponential size, and provide both
equational theories

(ET)
and
refinement theories
, for
reasoning on equivalences and entailment of representations. Requirement

(ET)
is inherently computational and is strongly tied to the possibility of
automated verification.

To this end, the core intellectual challenge is to design a compact symbolic formalism for hybrid classical/quantum processes. A particular obstacle is to design a tractable way of representing measurement, the induced set of potentially exponentially many alternative projections, and the evolution of the probability distributions of those exponentially many states in a way that avoids exponential calculations in practice.

A discussion of prior attempts is given in

Section

2
. In summary, the standard
density
operator representation

(
63
;
95
;
80
)
(
2^{n}\times 2^{n}

positive self-adjoint matrices) from physics does not scale well
with the number of qubits
n
and is unnatural for computational features such as
quantum control.

Perhaps the most successful correction to density operators to
include classical data is the
cq-states

(
36
)
where a hybrid
memory is described by pairs
(\sigma,\rho)
of a classical state
\sigma
and
a quantum state
\rho
(described as a (partial) density operator) occurring
with probability
\mathrm{tr}(\rho)
. However, cq-states still suffer from the
exponential blowup in the size of the density operators (
CT
), lack
efficient equational theories to equate two states without computing them
entirely (
ET
), and, by erasing global phases, make it impossible to
express quantum control compositionally (
QC
). This last feature is
essential to enable a modular analysis of programs, where language constructs
receive a functional interpretation.

The
ZX
calculus

(
26
;
25
)
, on the other hand,
is efficient for analysis and optimization but is not suitable for modeling program
constructs and hybrid specifications. Finally, (plain)

path-sums

(
3
;
4
)
provide compact symbolic
representations with good equational theories, but are limited to unitary
operations and do not support hybrid features.

Insights.

To address these challenges, we
extend the path-sum
(
3
;
4
)

symbolic representation of quantum superposition to more involved structures where quantum
superposition and probability distributions are interleaved.
In path-sums, the unitary evolution of a quantum
system from a state
A
to a state
B
is given by a symbolic weighted sum of
all possible paths from
A
to
B
. For example, the Hadamard gate
H
applied
to a basis state
|x\rangle
is represented as

\left\langle\frac{xy}{2},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot|y\rangle\right\rangle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{e}^{2\pi i\frac{xy}{2}}|y\rangle
, where
y
indexes the paths that are being
summed.
Notice how the phase term
\frac{xy}{2}
encodes the matrix for
H,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\right)
with indexes
x
and
y
. We extend this formalism by interpreting measurement as
muting the symbolic expression for qubits
|y\rangle
into a classical bit which we
denote
[y]
to obtain
\left\langle\frac{xy}{2},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot[y]\right\rangle
.
This
[\cdot]
decoration instructs us to partition the set of paths according
to the value of
y
. In this case, we obtain
two
worlds
1
1

1

The reference to alternative
worlds
is inspired by
the many-world interpretation of quantum
mechanics
(
33
;
31
)
:
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[0]
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[1]
no longer in superposition
and each happening with probability
\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}
. The term
[y]
also serves as a natural representation of classical data
(
HD
) that is accessible to the program, something which is missing
from standard path-sums and density operators
(
63
;
95
;
80
)
.

In
order to handle information about the structure of world splittings, hybrid
path-sums do not only explicitly encode alternative measurement results, but
also preserve this information by storing the entire history of classical
values. As such, the branching structure of worlds is preserved by (non-unitary)
operations over classical data, such as resetting a register to a constant.

To overcome the potential induced accumulation of non-relevant information, the
formalism is also provided with abstractions and mechanisms enabling
an automated or user-controlled filtering of the relevant information out of the
representation (
Section

7
).

Hence, our solution
(i) is expressive enough to represent classical
(CC)
and quantum
(QC)
control and to derive standard algorithm specifications and safe information discard; and (ii) preserves the compactness (
CT
), tractability (
ET
), and elegance of original path-sums for unitary programs [3].

Contributions

Building on the previous ideas, we set the basis for a fully integrated hybrid
deductive verification framework.
To anchor it, we introduce H
Qbricks
, a language for hybrid classical-quantum programs
(
Section

5
) that supports measurement (
M
),
quantum and classical instructions (
HD
) with bounded loops, as control a both quantum (
QC
) and classical
(
CC
) conditions.
Based on this language, we present the following contributions:

•

HPS
, a
compact (CT) symbolic representation
for hybrid
classical/quantum program states (
Section

6
) that extends the path-sum
formalism
(
3
)
to support measurement, classical variables
and hybrid control, and is provided with
concretizations
interpreting it in
terms of probabilistic hybrid classical/quantum state descriptions
(
Section

6.2
).
HPS
also soundly (
Theorem

7.1
)
extend the path-sum
equational theory
(
ET
), enabling rewriting and
simplification in the hybrid case. This equational theory is completed with
local reasoning rules
, enabling partial state assertions and information discarding.
Local reasoning widens the scope of patterns that can be handled by
equational reasoning. We show it in action in several case studies of interest in
Section

9
;

•

A symbolic execution framework for hybrid quantum programs based on
HPS
. It contains a full
forward propagation semantics
(
Section

8.1
) for H
Qbricks
programs that is sound relative to standard
(
36
)
density operators based semantics (
Theorem

8.1
), and a dedicated

core assertion language over hybrid states
(
Section

8.2
), with the corresponding rules to check them on
HPS
.
Notably, our assertion language can express hybrid state equivalence, hybrid predicate satisfaction and the probability of satisfying a given Boolean condition;

•

A
complete implementation
of this framework in a working prototype symbolic executor for H
Qbricks
programs,
and a comparative experimental evaluation over
5
involved cases from the literature (
Section

10
). These case studies illustrate the main aspects of our framework. In addition, they showcase the flexibility of the method.
Furthermore, it turns out that our approach scales significantly better than state-of-the-art tools on examples where comparison is available.

In short, we provide a

foundational framework for the
automated formal
verification of hybrid quantum programs.
We propose a symbolic representation together with a reasoning mechanism that
is sufficient for deriving textbook specifications for standard computation
patterns from the literature (e.g., effective transmission of quantum states
through teleportation, probability of success for QPE, Repeat Until Success
schemes), and show the potential of this formalism by showcasing its

•

scalability:
this is the first solution to provide symbolic
execution for hybrid classical/quantum computations scaling to thousands of
qubits on different standard case studies (teleportation, phase estimation, error correction);

•

flexibility: the formalism can handle diverse features such as
high-level classically controlled operations (Bounded Repeat-Until-Success), quantum information processing (Quantum Teleportation), noise modeling and error correction, as well as gate to gate quantum circuit composition (Phase Estimation), whereas in the literature these features are investigated in
separated developments.

In future works, we intend to build upon the present contribution to handle essential hybrid quantum features still out of scope, such as unbounded control loops,
approximative correctness, deepened error correction analysis, integration of complex operations over classical data and expected values/resource estimation.

Prior Attempts

.

)

(

)

(

)

(

;

)

(

)

(

;

)

CT:

Compactness

Formal verification of quantum programs has grown in interest for the last 5
to 10 years, with the emergence of a sustainable body of solutions. In

Table

1
, we present some notable symbolic representations of quantum
and/or hybrid processes. They are compared to one another and to our
HPS

solution in their ability to address the different items mentioned in the ‘Goals and challenges’ paragraph above.

As introduced in
Section

1
, the
density operator formalism
,
together with the superoperator interpretation of quantum processes involving
measurement
(
80
)
, is the standard representation for quantum
states, and as such it serves as a reference
formalism for many quantum information analysis tools
such as purity, trace
distance, entropy, etc. (see Nielsen and Chuang
(
66
)
, part III).

It also provides a robust reference for
operational semantics. Nevertheless, density operators provide no means to
simplify, abstract, or compactify in any way the
exponential explosion

inherent to quantum program execution. Therefore, density operators alone fail
to provide an automatable and tractable solution for analyzing programs. To fill this
gap, they need to be provided with an additional symbolic layer targeting their
representation.

To do so, a recently explored
solution
(
12
;
95
;
90
)
relies on the
formalized usage of the labeled Dirac notation
(
32
)
. This extra
symbolism provides more compact and intuitive data representations and
specifications, as well as additional reasoning rules. In particular, some of
these rules reason over state descriptions instead of density operators. One
therefore encodes specifications about an
n
-qubit system in a
2^{n}

dimensional object, a quadratic reduction with regard to the
2^{2n}
density
operator based representation.

As a side effect, our current proposition also generalizes this use of
symbolic state description encoded in density operators/cq-states
(
Definition

6.4
).

An additional limitation of density operators is the use of complex vector
spaces with unnatural encodings of classical information. Solutions either
encode booleans in tensor combinations
(
80
)
or restrict the
quantum processes under consideration to those that do not require explicit reference
to classical data
(
92
;
95
)
. The cq-state
(
36
)

extension of the density operator semantics overcomes this last limitation.
However, it still lacks compactness (uses exponentially sized density operators),
a functorial interpretation of quantum control, and theory for simplification.

ZX calculus

(
26
;
25
)
is an
alternative
representation for instances of quantum processes and
circuits equipped with a very powerful rewriting theory. The derived
PyZX
(
54
)
tool, e.g., is capable of performing verification for circuits
that feature thousands of gates in seconds, making it a powerful tool for
equivalence checking and/or circuit optimization for the design of compilation tasks. As a drawback with respect to our purpose, ZX calculus does not
represent process inputs nor outputs, be they hybrid, classical, or quantum
data. Therefore, it is not suitable for representing and evaluating specifications
in terms of the probability of a memory being in a state satisfying a given
condition. Also, the formalism is very low-level and misses programming
constructs such as iterations or quantum control. Raising ZX verification
techniques to the parametrized case was recently addressed through the

scalable ZX
extension
(
17
;
16
)
, demonstrating standard algorithm modeling but
still suffering from the difficulty of connecting an actual program to a diagrammatic
representation.

In the recent
Qafny
proposition
(
62
;
23
)
,
the user chooses between several provided
state representations to carry out a correctness proof. All these representations are
state-based, with similarities to the path-sum formalism. The solution enables
to formalize unitary computations with terminal measurement.
However, it cannot handle feedback
from the measurement results to the rest of the execution (classical control).
Therefore, hybrid programs with proper interactions between classical and quantum data
(e.g., teleportation, error correction, repeat until success) are not supported.

Our present development is deeply inspired by the
path-sum
calculus

(
3
)
, which combines compactness with an equational theory with good computational properties and which is complete for the unitary Clifford fragment. In recent years, it has also
served as a seminal basis for different further extensions such as
parameterization
(
18
;
29
)
, unbalanced sums

(
4
;
29
)
, or
measurement
(
88
)
.

However, while the unbalanced treatment from
(
4
)
seems to
pave the way for the representation of quantum control, a possibility that has not yet been exploited. Similarly, the
treatment of quantum measurement in
(
88
)
(via “discarded
qubits”) generates a collapse of the quantum state system, but measurement results
are never stored. Therefore, once again, this formalism cannot handle classical
control based on measurement outcomes. In
particular, as in the case of Qafny, it is not sufficient to handle teleportation, error correction, or classically controlled loops.
Finally, while these features partly appear in different solutions, there exists
no single proposition simultaneously offering all of them to meet the requirements from
Table

1
.
In this article, we provide such a unified solution based on path-sums.

3.
Background: Quantum Algorithms and Programs

Quantum Computing.
We adopt the quantum coprocessor
model
(
59
)
where a classical computer performs classical
computation while delegating to a quantum processing unit (QPU) the quantum
parts of the computation. The combined flow of classical and quantum operations
is described by
hybrid circuits
. On the QPU,
n
-bit vector are replaced
by
n
-
qubit
registers which can be in a linear combination
(
superposition
) of all
2^{n}
bit strings of length
n
(
basis
states
). Each basis state
b
is weighted by its
amplitude

\alpha_{b}\in\texttt{c}
, so that the state of the register becomes

|\psi\rangle=\sum_{b\in{\{0,1\}}^{n}}\alpha_{b}|b\rangle
.
The vector
|\psi\rangle
cannot be read in full; instead, it is

measured
resulting in an outcome
b
with probability

|\alpha_{b}|^{2}
, implying the
normalisation
of the state:
\||\psi\rangle\|=1
. After measurement the state changes (
collapses
) to

|b\rangle
. A typical execution flow consists of
initializing
a
qubit into a basis state, applying
unitary operations
(linear maps
preserving the inner product), measuring the state, and using the measurement
result for further classical computing and control. For a more comprehensive
introduction to quantum information and quantum computing, we refer the desirous
reader to
(
66
)
. In this work, we focus largely on the
interplay of quantum and classical computation, a notorious blind spot in the
literature which has only recently gained some attention in the context of
low-level languages
(
2
;
27
;
40
)
.

The Path-Sum Representation.

Our proposition relies on the path-sum
representation
(
3
;
4
;
89
;
88
;
29
)
,
a discrete version of Feynman’s path integrals
(
37
)
. In it,
the evolution of a quantum system from a state
A
to a state
B
is described
by a symbolic sum of possible paths from
A
to
B
. Simply put,
a path-sum is a triplet
\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle
of symbolic expressions in boolean
variables
y
called
path variables
and interpreted as indexing the
set of superposed paths the system takes to evolve from an original state

|x\rangle
to a final state
|z\rangle
, such that, with
r
being the
number of variables in

\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle
2
2

2

Path-sum notations vary significantly within the literature

|x\rangle\mapsto|z\rangle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}^{r}\texttt{s}(x,y)e^{2i\pi\texttt{p}(x,y)}|\texttt{o}(x,y)\rangle

As representations of unitaries, path-sums can be composed sequentially
\circ

and in parallel
\otimes
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}^{\prime},\texttt{s}^{\prime}\cdot\texttt{o}^{\prime}\right\rangle\circ\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\texttt{p}+\texttt{p}^{\prime}[\texttt{o}/x],\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{s}^{\prime}[\texttt{o}/x]\cdot\texttt{o}^{\prime}[\texttt{o}/x]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}^{\prime},\texttt{s}^{\prime}\cdot\texttt{o}^{\prime}\right\rangle\otimes\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\texttt{p}+\texttt{p}^{\prime},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{s}^{\prime}\cdot\texttt{o}\otimes\texttt{o}^{\prime}\right\rangle

As such, we can describe complex unitaries from primitive ones (
gates
),
such as the Hadamard
H
which introduces superposition, or the control not
(
CNOT
) which entangles two qubits by flipping the target when the control
is 1:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:|x_{0}\rangle\mapsto\left\langle\frac{x_{0}y_{0}}{2},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot|y_{0}\rangle\right\rangle\quad

CNOT

\displaystyle:|x_{0}x_{1}\rangle\mapsto\left\langle 0,1\cdot|x_{0}(x_{0}\oplus x_{1})\rangle\right\rangle

However, path-sum representations are not unique. For instance, as a linear
operator
H^{2}=I
, but the path-sum representation of
H^{2}
is

\left\langle\frac{y_{0}y_{1}}{2}+\frac{y_{0}\texttt{o}}{2},\frac{1}{2}\cdot|y_{1}\rangle\right\rangle
, manifestly
different from the identity
\left\langle 0,1\cdot|\texttt{o}\rangle\right\rangle
. As such, rewrite rules
(
Section

7
) such as the
HH
below are required to detect these
equivalent
HPS
and simplify them.

\left\langle\texttt{p}+\frac{y_{0}y_{1}}{2}+\frac{y_{0}\texttt{o}}{2},\frac{1}{2}\texttt{s}\cdot|y_{1}\rangle\right\rangle\xrightarrow{HH}\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot|\texttt{o}\rangle\right\rangle

.
Quantum teleportation circuit

Consider the standard quantum teleportation
protocol
(
9
;
66
)

which moves the state
|x\rangle_{\psi}
of a qubit
\psi
into a qubit
b
.
Teleportation is an essential building block in quantum information
transmission
(
13
)
, Measurement-based Quantum Computation
(MBQC)
(
74
)
, and more.

The hybrid circuit for teleportation is given in
Fig.

1
and we describe the induced hybrid state evolution in

Table

2
, using (i) a pseudo-formal description of
the state, (ii) the path-sum abstraction as provided in
(
3
)
,
(iii) the cq-state
(
36
)
representation, and (iv) the
HPS
formalism,
subject of the current article.

The costs indicated in the top row of the table express the size of the representation
for the teleportation example parametrized with the number
n
of teleported
qubits. Despite being seemingly specific to teleportation, this example is
actually representative of the sizes of the various representations in typical
applications.
Furthermore,
while the
HPS
representation is extensible, by linearity, to any
state of the Hilbert space, this is not the case for the representation
in cq-states and the corresponding column is only valid for basis
vectors.

0&0&0&0&x&0&x&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot|x\rangle_{\psi}|y_{0}\rangle_{a}|0\rangle_{b}\right\rangle_{\{\texttt{y}_{0}\}}

\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{9}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

*\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}\overline{x}&0\\
0&x\\
\end{array}\right)

\begin{array}[]{l}\langle|x\rangle_{b}\rangle_{\emptyset}\otimes\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{2}\cdot[y_{1}]_{\psi_{c}}[y_{0}\oplus x]_{a_{c}}\right\rangle_{\{\texttt{y}_{0},y_{1}\}}\\
\preccurlyeq_{\texttt{r}}\langle|x\rangle_{b}\rangle_{\emptyset}\\
\end{array}

Unitary evolution.

At step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, Alice and Bob initialize each a qubit in state
0
. Then
(step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
), Alice applies the Hadamard gate on her qubit
a
,
setting it in the superposition
|+\rangle_{a}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|0\rangle+|1\rangle)
(
Section

3
). In the path-sum column, this (branching)
superposition manifests by the introduction of a fresh free boolean

path-variable

y_{0}
, encoding the two new branches
|0\rangle_{a}
and

|1\rangle_{a}
as
|y_{0}\rangle_{a}
. Step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{3}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
then consists of a sequence of

cnot
gates, interpreted as an addition modulo 2 (written
\oplus
) in the
target wire (see
Section

3
again).
At step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{4}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, another Hadamard gate further
splits each path, encoded by the introduction of
another boolean path variable
y_{1}
. So far, the column
HPS
is identical to
the standard path-sum column, as the circuit is purely quantum.

Measurements and Corrections.

Next, measurement is performed on the wire
\psi
(Step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{5}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
). This
operation introduces non-determinism, according to the Born rule
(see
Section

3
): for each possible measurement result
m\in\{0,1\}
, the
initial state of the register is projected onto the subspaces characterized by

|m\rangle_{\psi}
. Standard path-sums
(
3
)
cannot support this
non-determinism, so we must use an extensive description of the probability
distribution as in the first two columns of line
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{5}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Importantly, however, while the set of paths is split into these two measurement scenarios, each path is individually preserved by the
measurement.
This is
harnessed by
HPS
which encodes the measurement as a transfer of the measured
quantum value
|y_{1}\rangle_{\psi}
into a classical memory cell
{[y_{1}]}_{\psi_{c}}

to denote that this expression
y_{1}
must now be used to partition paths.

Step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{6}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
then corresponds to the second measurement operation
similar to Step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{5}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and Step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{7}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
) encodes
unitary correction
\oplus
which performs a bit flip, i.e. transforming the state

|x\rangle
into
|x\oplus 1\rangle
(resp. phase shift
Z
transforming
state
|x\rangle
into
{(-1)}^{x}|x\rangle
) on the wire
b
, controlled by
measurement result
a_{c}
(resp.
{\psi_{c}}
). At that stage (Step

{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{8}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
), in the
HPS
representation, the wire
b
clearly holds the value

|x\rangle
showing that the qubit has indeed been transferred to Bob,
regardless of the measurement results.

Distribution and Discard.

Finally, step
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{9}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
illustrates the usage of

HPS
as specifications.
It consists of two steps: first, the
HPS
is
separated into

\langle 0,1\cdot|x\rangle_{b}\rangle_{\emptyset}
and
\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{2}\cdot{[y_{1}]}_{\psi_{c}}{[y_{0}\oplus x]}_{a_{c}}\right\rangle_{\{\texttt{y}_{0},y_{1}\}}
according to the
construction of the tensor product,
then, the irrelevant part of the state is formally discarded
(see
Section

7
) leaving us with the original state

\left\langle 0,1\cdot|x\rangle_{b}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}
now on the qubit
b
.

5.
Hybrid Quantum Programs: The H
Qbricks
Language

Consider the hybrid imperative language H
Qbricks
. It supports unitary gates

G\in\ \mathcal{G}
on qubits
Q
, measurement, quantum and classical conditioning,
bounded for loops, and oracles
f
applied on classical data
C
. The
H
Qbricks
language is given by a simply typed
\lambda
-calculus with seven
base types:
QReg
,
CReg
,
QBool
,
CBool
,
Int
,
QProg
, and
CProg
, all of
which are inductive
, and with
QProg
being a subtype of
CProg
corresponding to unitary programs.

Definition 0 (H
Qbricks
(
\mathcal{G}
)).

The grammar of the seven inductive base types is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\text{QReg},\texttt{Q}&:=&x_{\texttt{q}}\mid\texttt{Q}[\texttt{i}:]\mid\texttt{Q}[:\texttt{i}]\mid\texttt{Q}[\texttt{i}:\texttt{j}]\mid\texttt{Q}[\texttt{i}]&\\
\text{CReg},\texttt{C},\texttt{C}_{1},\texttt{C}_{2}&:=&x_{\texttt{c}}\mid\texttt{C}[\texttt{i}:]\mid\texttt{C}[:\texttt{i}]\mid\texttt{C}[\texttt{i}:\texttt{j}]\mid\texttt{C}[\texttt{i}]&\\
\text{QBool},\text{qb},\text{qb}_{1},\text{qb}_{2}&:=&x_{\text{qb}}\mid 0_{\text{qb}}\mid 1_{\text{qb}}\mid\texttt{Q}\mid\text{qb}_{1}\oplus\text{qb}_{2}\mid\text{qb}_{1}\land\text{qb}_{2}\mid\text{qb}?\left(\text{qb}_{1};\text{qb}_{2}\right)&\\
\text{CBool},\texttt{cb},\texttt{cb}_{1},\texttt{cb}_{2}&:=&x_{\texttt{cb}}\mid 0_{\texttt{cb}}\mid 1_{\texttt{cb}}\mid\texttt{C}\mid\texttt{cb}_{1}\oplus\texttt{cb}_{2}\mid\texttt{cb}_{1}\land\texttt{cb}_{2}\mid\texttt{cb}?\left(\texttt{cb}_{1};\texttt{cb}_{2}\right)&\\
\text{Int},\texttt{i},\texttt{j}&:=&x_{\texttt{i}}\mid\texttt{const}\mid\texttt{i}+\texttt{j}\mid\texttt{i}*\texttt{j}\mid\texttt{i}^{\texttt{j}}\mid\#(\texttt{C})\mid\#(\texttt{Q})&\\
\text{QProg},\texttt{qP},\texttt{qP}^{\prime}&:=&\textbf{Skip }\mid\texttt{qP};\texttt{qP}^{\prime}\mid\textbf{For }x_{n}^{I}=i,j\textbf{ do }\texttt{qP}\textbf{ done}\mid\hskip 0.0pt\textbf{If }\text{qb}\textbf{ then }\texttt{qP}\textbf{ else }\texttt{qP}^{\prime}\textbf{ end}\\
&&\mid\textbf{Apply}\ \texttt{G}(\texttt{Q})&\\
\text{Prog},\texttt{Pr},\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}&:=&\textbf{Init}\ \texttt{Q}\mid\texttt{qP}\mid\texttt{Pr};\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}\mid\textbf{For }x_{\texttt{c}}^{\texttt{I}}=\texttt{i},\texttt{j}\textbf{ do }\texttt{Pr}\textbf{ done}&\\
&&\mid\textbf{If }\texttt{cb}\textbf{ then }\texttt{Pr}\textbf{ else }\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}\textbf{ end}\mid\textbf{Measure}(\texttt{Q},\texttt{C})\mid\texttt{C}_{1}:=f(\texttt{C}_{2})&\end{array}

where, for any type identifier
T
,
x_{\texttt{T}}
is a variable of
type
T
and
const
is a constant integer.

In explicit terms,
QReg
and
CReg
are quantum and classical registers and

QBool
and
CBool
are booleans that depend on values of quantum and
classical registers, respectively. This distinction is important when it comes to
control, also explaining the need to isolate a subtype
QProg
of
Prog
,
which corresponds to purely quantum (unitary) programs, the only
programs that can be quantum controlled. The
ternary construct
a?\left(b;c\right)
in both boolean types is equivalent to
((a\oplus 1)\wedge b)\oplus(a\wedge c)
. In this article, this ternary
notation is also used in
HPS
more broadly.

5.1.
Intuition of the Syntax

The intuition is as follows:
Skip
does nothing,
\texttt{Pr};\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}

performs
Pr
followed by
\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}
,
The bounds
of
For
loop are parameters of the program and cannot make reference to
values in the memory; i.e. loops can always be unraveled after instantiation of
program parameters, but before execution.

If

b

then

Pr

else
\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}
end
performs quantum or hybrid conditioning
when
Pr
and
\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}
are unitary, and classical conditioning otherwise,

\textbf{Measure}(\texttt{Q},\texttt{C})
measures
Q
storing the result in
C
.
H
Qbricks
can embed purely classical computation via oracles
f
in
\texttt{C}_{1}:=f(\texttt{C}_{2})
.

As for unitaries,
they are applied by command
Apply

G
, where
\texttt{G}\in\mathcal{G}
. In the rest of this paper, we instantiate
\mathcal{G}
as the set
Z^{*}=\{H,X\}\cup{\{Z_{k}\}}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, containing the Hadamard gate, the

not

X
gate and rotation gates
Z_{k}
on the Z-axis of angle

\frac{2i\pi}{2^{k}}

given by matrix
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&e^{\frac{2i\pi}{2^{k}}}\end{pmatrix}
.

Finally, note that the syntax allows using entire registers of more than one bit/qubit as
booleans. In this case, the boolean value represented is the conjunction of the values of the bits/qubits
in the register, reflecting the typical use of multiple control in quantum circuits.

5.2.
Comparison with State-of-the-Art Formally Verified Languages

Quantum formal verification
solutions
(
23
;
62
;
63
)
are often built upon
a
while
language where the measurement is never separated from the
control that is immediately performed on it. This restriction is highly limiting
on the possible implementations (e.g., teleportation or error correction are not
supported). Interestingly,
qhl-cv

(
36
)
extends
this approach with classical variables. We use
qhl-cv
as an anchor for the
soundness of our semantics (
Theorem

8.1
).
By explicitly presenting H
Qbricks
, we aim
to highlight: (i) the benefits from embedding in a classical host language. It
enables us to integrate pure classical computation results in H
Qbricks
using oracles;
and (ii) the fact that, in H
Qbricks
, quantum data are
never addressed directly, but only through operational commands. This provides a

correct-by-construction
guarantee over potential violations of quantum
mechanical postulates (non-cloning theorem and unitarity) without requiring a
linear type system such as in,
e.g.,
(
79
;
77
;
45
)
.

5.3.
Discussion

In H
Qbricks
, arbitrary classical computations are introduced as
black-box oracles stored in classical memory cells. We envision to handle these oracles with logical function names
with adequate axioms ("logical oracles", in a sense) expressed in standard theories over classical data (e.g., integers or bit
vectors), and then to delegate the associated reasoning to the existing classical programming verification ecosystem.

Note also that, as opposed
to
qhl-cv

(
36
)
, H
Qbricks
does not support unbounded
loops. Handling them requires an extension of the path-sum semantics with infinite sums over paths
and some additional structure for the set of path-sums, so as to properly symbolically encode invariants.
We consider these aspects as
orthogonal to the scope of this paper and leave them for future work.

5.4.
Type System

The language is a typed
\lambda
-calculus with constructors for hybrid circuits
as (closed) terms of type Prog. The typing enforces some well-formedness
criteria and raises unitarity constraints or proof obligations on the go.
In order to address questions of unitarity under quantum control, we introduce a
subtype QProg of Prog for purely unitary programs, the only ones allowed to be
quantum controlled.

Note that the quantum control rule (
Q-if
below) raises a syntactic precondition (ensuring non-intersection of control and target qubits and introduced with
\vdash^{*}
).
Additional syntactic conditions of well-formedness include applying gates only to allocated (initialized)
registers, not re-initializing already initialized qubits, and having the same memory allocation
profile (sets of allocated/unallocated registers) in either branch of a conditional.
In most standard cases (including in our case studies implementation, see
Section

9
), simple syntactic checks are sufficient for verifying these conditions.
In future advanced usage, corner cases might still appear that will require discharging these verifications to external SMT-solvers.

The main language constructors rules are as follows
(completed with an exhaustive
rule exposition in Appendix
B
).

\Gamma\vdash p:\text{QProg}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}QProg}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash p:\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash q:\text{QReg}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Init}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash\textbf{Init}(q):\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash q:\text{QReg}

\Gamma\vdash c:\text{CReg}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Measure}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash\textbf{Measure}(q,c):\text{Prog}

U\in\mathcal{G}

\Gamma\vdash q:\text{QReg}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Unitary}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash\textbf{Apply}\ U(q):\text{QProg}

\Gamma\vdash c_{1}:\text{CReg}

\Gamma\vdash c_{2}:\text{CReg}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Classical}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash c_{1}:=f(\lceil c_{2}\rceil):\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash p:\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash q:\text{Prog}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Seq}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash p;q:\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash i:\text{Int}

\Gamma\vdash j:\text{Int}

\Gamma\vdash p:\text{Prog}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}For}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash\textbf{For }x_{n}^{I}=i,j\textbf{ do }\{p\}:\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash b:\text{CBool}

\Gamma\vdash p:\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash q:\text{Prog}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}C-if}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash\textbf{If }b\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end}:\text{Prog}

\Gamma\vdash^{*}\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}(p)\cap\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}(b)=\emptyset

\Gamma\vdash b:\text{Bool}

\Gamma\vdash p:\text{QProg}

\Gamma\vdash q:\text{QProg}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Q-if}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash\textbf{If }b\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end}:\text{QProg}

The usual
\lambda
-calculus typing rules are also included. Note also that,
since we do not address quantum data directly, but only via operational commands, we do not need linear types to ensure unitarity and our type
system admits weakening and contraction.

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Ax}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma,x:T\vdash x:T

\Gamma\vdash\Delta

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Weak}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma,x:T\vdash t:U

\lambda

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:T\to U

\Gamma\vdash f:T\to U

\Gamma\vdash t:T

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}@}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\Gamma\vdash ft:U

5.5.
Standard Semantics

H\textsc{Qbricks}
has well-established semantics in terms of density
operators
(
63
;
95
;
80
)
(without classical data) or
cq-states
(
36
)
(with classical data). In the cq-state semantics, a
hybrid memory is described as a probability distribution over pairs
(\sigma,\rho)
of a classical state
\sigma
and a quantum state
\rho
(described as a
density operator).
3
3

3

Technically, it is a map
\sigma\mapsto\mathbb{P}(\sigma,\rho)\rho
into
partial
density operators with norm
\leq 1
A quantum input
|x\rangle_{\texttt{reg}}
is represented as the cq-state
(-,|x\rangle\!\!\ \langle x|)
,
where
-
is the empty classical state. A denotational semantics can then be
defined as such:

\displaystyle\llbracket U\rrbracket\langle\sigma,\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma,U\rho U^{\dagger}\rangle

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{Measure}(\texttt{Q},\texttt{C})\rrbracket\langle\sigma,\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma[\texttt{C}:=0],|0\rangle_{\texttt{Q}}\langle 0|_{\texttt{Q}}\rho|0\rangle_{\texttt{Q}}\langle 0|_{\texttt{Q}}\rangle+\langle\sigma[\texttt{C}:=1],|1\rangle_{\texttt{Q}}\langle 1|_{\texttt{Q}}\rho|1\rangle_{\texttt{Q}}\langle 1|_{\texttt{Q}}\rangle

\displaystyle\llbracket\texttt{C}_{1}:=f(\texttt{C}_{2})\rrbracket\langle\sigma,\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma[\texttt{C}_{1}:=f(\sigma(\texttt{C}_{2}))],\rho\rangle

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{If }\texttt{cb}\textbf{ then }\texttt{Pr}\textbf{ else }\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}\textbf{ end}\rrbracket\langle\sigma,\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\llbracket\texttt{Pr}\rrbracket\langle\sigma,\rho\rangle&\text{if }\sigma\models\texttt{cb}\\
\llbracket\texttt{Pr}^{\prime}\rrbracket\langle\sigma,\rho\rangle&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

This semantics is lacking compactness and symbolism (
Section

1
), so we
instead provide alternative semantics based on path-sums that surmounts these
limitations, and we show that it is sound with respect to these usual
cq-state/density operator semantics.

6.
Hybrid Path Sums (HPS)

6.1.
Formal Definition

Hybrid path-sums extend unbalanced path-sums to the hybrid case. They manipulate
hybrid quantum-classical memories, that we first introduce:

Definition 0 (Hybrid memory).

A
quantum memory
is a finite map from a set of
quantum addresses
to symbolic Boolean
expressions, while a
classical memory
is a list of finite maps from a set of
classical addresses
to symbolic Boolean
expressions, storing the history of classical value computation along the execution. We often refer to a hybrid memory (a
pair of quantum and classical memories) simply as a
memory
.

Only the head of the list in a classical memory is accessible by programs while the
remainder merely contains past values necessary to track the structure
of world splittings.
One can visualize a classical memory as a two-dimensional table featuring
addresses (space) on one axis and time on the other. Due to initializations
and Discard operations (
Section

7
), some cells of the table are
‘holes’ denoted
-
. If a pair of classical memories
C_{1}
and

C_{2}
, seen as partial maps from pairs
(\text{address},\text{time})
to
boolean expressions, have disjoint domains, they can be combined in parallel
as
C_{1}\otimes C_{2}
.

Running example 1.

Aside is the evolution of classical memory in
teleportation (
Table

2
).
This memory stores the result of measurements at steps
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{5}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(time
t_{1}
)
and
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{6}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(time
t_{2}
) in addresses
\bm{\psi}
and
\bm{a}
respectively.
We note that pasts (non-head entries) of classical memories were omitted from

Table

2
since no overwriting of classical addresses occurs.

Table 3
.
Evolution of the classical memory.

\begin{array}[]{ccc|c}\hline\cr\textbf{Addresses}&\bm{\psi}&\bm{a}&\textbf{Time step}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr&y_{1}&y_{0}\oplus x&t_{2}:6,7\\
&y_{1}&\_&t_{1}:5\\
&\_&\_&t_{0}:1,2,3,4\end{array}

Definition 0 (HPS).

A hybrid path-sum (
HPS
)
\texttt{i}\mapsto\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}
is given
by:

•

An
input signature
: a hybrid memory containing boolean

input variables

\{x_{i}\}
or constants.

•

A
path support

su
: a set of boolean
path
variables

\{y_{j}\}
.

•

An
output signature

o
: a hybrid memory
containing boolean polynomials in
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
.

•

A
phase polynomial

\texttt{p}\in\mathbb{D}[x_{i},y_{j}]
in
x_{i}

and
y_{j}
over the ring of dyadics
\mathbb{D}=\{\frac{n}{2^{m}}:n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

•

A
scalar function

s
over
x_{i}
and

y_{j}
given by the following
constructible
numbers

(
30
)
grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rclc}\textsc{Scalar},\texttt{s}&:=&\frac{\texttt{i}_{1}}{\texttt{i}_{2}}\mid\sqrt{\texttt{s}}\ \mid\operatorname{\texttt{cos}}\ \texttt{a}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{sin}}\ \texttt{a}\mid\texttt{s}_{1}+_{r}\texttt{s}_{2}\mid-r\mid\texttt{s}_{1}*_{r}\texttt{s}_{2}\mid\frac{1}{\texttt{s}}\mid x_{i}\mid y_{j}&\\
\textsc{Angle},\texttt{a}&:=&\frac{2\pi\texttt{i}_{1}}{2^{\texttt{i}_{2}}}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{arccos}}\ \texttt{s}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{arcsin}}\ \texttt{s}\mid\texttt{i}*_{a}\texttt{a}\mid\texttt{b}*_{a}\texttt{a}\mid\texttt{a}_{1}+_{a}\texttt{a}_{2}\mid-\texttt{a}&\\
\end{array}

Notation 1.

A
Register

r
is an array of indexed memory addresses

\texttt{r}[i]
.
|x\rangle_{\texttt{qu},i}
(resp.
{[x]}_{\texttt{c},i}
) is a quantum
(resp. classical) data cell with
address

(\texttt{qu}[i])
(resp.
(\texttt{c}[i])
),
index

i
, and
containing

x_{0}
. To address a vector of
several variables, we use a capital font:
X
designates a vector of input
variables while
Y
designates a vector of path variables.
\lceil\texttt{reg}\rceil_{i}
is
the expression contained at
\texttt{reg}[i]
.. In the case of classical register, only
the topmost value is used.
The quantum and classical parts of the output
o
are
denoted by
\texttt{o}_{\texttt{Qu}}
and
\texttt{o}_{\texttt{Cl}}
, respectively. The height of
\texttt{o}_{\texttt{Cl}}
is
its
age
and is also the
age
of the
HPS
. A fully
instantiated
\texttt{o}_{\texttt{Cl}}
is called a
history
and denoted
\eta
. When

reg
is of size 1, we write
\texttt{reg}=\texttt{reg}[0]
. Finally,
i
and
su
are
almost always implicit.

6.2.
Probability Derivation and Concretizations

In a hybrid path sum
\texttt{h}=\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}
, each possible (history)
\eta
for
\texttt{o}_{\text{cl}}

determines a world where
\eta
describes the past evolution of the classical memory. Each such
world is associated to a state
of the quantum registers. We can therefore define the vector-map of an

HPS
as the following.

Definition 0 (Concretization I: Vector map of an
HPS
).

Let
\texttt{h}=\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}
, then:

\mathcal{V}:\eta\mapsto\sum_{y\in 2^{\texttt{su}},\texttt{o}_{\text{cl}}(y)=\eta}\texttt{s}(y)\cdot e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}(y)}|\texttt{o}_{\text{qu}}\rangle

Running example 2.

At step 6 of
Table

2
, we have
PS_{6}=\left\langle\frac{y_{1}x}{2},\frac{1}{2}\cdot|y_{0}\rangle_{b}{[y_{1}]}_{\bm{\psi}_{c}}{[y_{0}\oplus x]}_{\bm{a}{{}_{c}}}\right\rangle_{\{\texttt{y}_{0},y_{1}\}}

\mathcal{V}(PS_{6}):\eta\in{\{0,1\}}^{\bm{\psi{{}_{c}},a_{c}}}\mapsto\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}y_{0}=\eta(\bm{a}_{c})\oplus 1\\
y_{1}=\eta(\bm{\psi}_{c})\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{1}{2}e^{\pi i(y_{1}x)}|y_{0}\rangle_{b}=\frac{{(-1)}^{x\eta(\bm{\psi}_{c})}}{2}|\eta(\bm{a}_{c})\oplus 1\rangle_{b}

This map anchors our
HPS
representation with concrete vectors of the
Hilbert space and is essential in discussing and proving the soundness of
both the equational theory (Section
7
) and symbolic
semantics (Section
8.1
), and in defining
semantics assertions over H
Qbricks
programs
(Section
8
). However, in practice, worlds
that differ only by a global phase are indistinguishable, so we also
introduce
\tilde{}\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}):\eta\mapsto\overline{\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)}
, the
equivalence class of
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)
modulo global phase.

Each history
\eta
is associated to a present value
\lceil\eta\rceil
of the
classical memory (top row) which is reached with probability
\texttt{proba}(\texttt{h})(c)=\sum_{\eta\mid\lceil\eta\rceil=c}|\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)|^{2}
. More generally, the probability of a predicate

\varphi
over the classical registers is
\texttt{proba}(\texttt{h})(\varphi)=\sum_{\eta\mid\lceil\eta\rceil\vdash\varphi}|\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)|^{2}
. The
norm
of

h
can then be defined as
|\texttt{h}|=\sqrt{\sum_{\eta}|\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)|^{2}}
.

To discuss the soundness of our symbolic execution with respect to the
cq-state
(
36
)
semantics, we also provide another concretization in terms
of cq-states.

Definition 0 (Concretization II: Density operator map of an
HPS
).

Let

\texttt{h}=\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}
Then:

\mathcal{DO}(\texttt{h}):c\mapsto\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1},y_{2}\in 2^{\texttt{su}},\texttt{o}_{\text{cl}}(y_{1})=\texttt{o}_{\text{cl}}(y_{2}),\lceil\texttt{o}_{\text{cl}}(y_{1})\rceil=c\end{smallmatrix}}\texttt{s}(y_{1})\texttt{s}(y_{2})\cdot e^{2\pi i(\texttt{p}(y_{1})-\texttt{p}(y_{2}))}|\texttt{o}_{\text{qu}}(y_{1})\rangle\!\!\ \langle\texttt{o}_{\text{qu}}(y_{2})|

This definition is equivalent to
\mathcal{DO}(\texttt{h})(c)=\sum_{\lceil\eta\rceil=c}|\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)\rangle\!\!\ \langle\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)|
, so
\mathcal{DO}(\texttt{h})
is definable from
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})
by a
non-invertible loss of past classical information. This equivalence also
highlights the following equality, holding for an
HPS

h
and history
\eta
:

\texttt{proba}(\texttt{h})(c)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{DO}(\texttt{h})(c))
.

6.3.
Additional Features of
HPS
and Unbalancement Supplies.

In
(
4
)
, M. Amy extends the original path-sum
framework
(
3
)
to
unbalanced
path-sums, with norms
varying along paths, and describes a complete equational theory for them, at the
expense of a potentially exponential size of terms. This is not our interest
here; instead, we focus on keeping
HPS
compact and practical for symbolic
execution and specification.

Hybrid path-sums eventually represent vectors in a vector space, so we would
wish to have a vector space structure over them. This is made possible by the
unbalancement feature, and grants us scalar multiplication and addition of
HPS
,
but also tensor products defined as follows.

\displaystyle r\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle y_{f}?\left(\texttt{p}_{1};\texttt{p}_{2}\right),y_{f}?\left(\Pi(\texttt{su}_{2}\setminus\texttt{su}_{1})rn_{1};\Pi(\texttt{su}_{1}\setminus\texttt{su}_{2})n_{2}\right)\cdot y_{f}?\left(\texttt{o}_{1};\texttt{o}_{2}\right)\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{1}\cup\texttt{su}_{2}\cup\{y_{f}\}}

\displaystyle\texttt{h}_{1}\pstens\texttt{h}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1}+\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}_{1}*\texttt{s}_{2}\cdot\texttt{o}_{1}\cup\texttt{o}_{2}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{1}\cup\texttt{su}_{2}}

Theorem 6.5.

Given
\texttt{h}_{1}
and
\texttt{h}_{2}
two
HPS
and
a
a constructible real number, we
have:

\mathcal{V}(a\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{1})=a\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})+\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{2})=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})

Decomposition over
\psadd
and
\otimes
corresponds to case analysis and
subsystem analysis respectively, and as such allow local reasoning both in
state space and in physical space. This is essential in practice, especially
under complex control flows.

Running example 3.

The path-sum at step 7 of
Table

2
can be decomposed over
\otimes
, with
this decomposition constituting step 8 of the same table.

\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{2}\cdot|x\rangle_{b}{[y_{1}]}_{\bm{\psi}}{[y_{0}\oplus x]}_{\bm{a}}\right\rangle_{\{\texttt{y}_{0},y_{1}\}}=\langle|x\rangle_{b}\rangle_{\emptyset}\otimes\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{2}\cdot{[y_{1}]}_{\bm{\psi}}{[y_{0}\oplus x]}_{\bm{a}}\right\rangle_{\{\texttt{y}_{0},y_{1}\}}

The ability to perform case analysis over paths is critical for quantum control,
a notorious blind spot of most prior attempts (
Section

2
). In fact, once
an
HPS

h
is split into
\texttt{h}_{b}\psadd\texttt{h}_{\lnot b}
with
\texttt{h}_{b}
(resp.

\texttt{h}_{\lnot b}
) satisfying (resp. not satisfying) a quantum or classical boolean
condition
b
, an
\textbf{if }b\textbf{ then }\texttt{Pr}_{1}\texttt{ else }\texttt{Pr}_{2}\textbf{ end}
can be simply executed
by executing
\texttt{Pr}_{1}
over
\texttt{h}_{b}
and
\texttt{Pr}_{2}
over
\texttt{h}_{\lnot b}

and recombining as
\llbracket\texttt{Pr}_{1}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}_{b})\psadd\llbracket\texttt{Pr}_{2}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}_{\lnot b})
(
Section

8.1
).

7.
Rewriting Rules for
HPS

We equip
HPS
with a rewrite system enabling reasoning about equivalence,
simplification, and specification of
HPS
. Concretely, we provide, an
equational theory
\equiv^{\texttt{p}}
for simplifying quantum interference patterns,

\equiv
which also identifies
HPS
up to undetectable global phase, and a
preorder
\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}
, compatible with
\equiv
to capture lossy transformations
such as discarding subsystems.

Path-Variable Reductions.

A first goal of simplification is to reduce the number of path-variables.
This is achieved by the rules (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}HH}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}PB}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Filter}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}CV}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) below, which extend those of
(
3
;
88
)
.
In these rules,
f
is a boolean expression over the input and path
variables,
\overline{f}
is its casting as an integer expression where, in
particular,
\overline{f_{1}\oplus f_{2}}=\overline{f_{1}}+\overline{f_{2}}-2\overline{f_{1}}\,\overline{f_{2}}
,
y
is a vector of boolean variables of size
\#y
,
\Pi y
is the product
y_{1}\cdots y_{\#{y}}
, and finally,

\mathfrak{S}({\{0,1\}}^{n})
is the set of bijections over
n
-bit strings,
expressed as boolean formulae over the bits. The Phase-Bisector
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}PB}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

rule computes the superposition of paths varying only by a phase, using Euler’s
formula
1+e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}_{2}}=2(\operatorname{\texttt{cos}}\pi\texttt{p}_{2})e^{2\pi i({\texttt{p}_{2}}/2)}
,

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Filter}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
eliminates null-amplitude paths, and
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}CV}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
allows changing
path-variables, i.e. the indexing of the paths. These three rules generalize
[HH], [Elim] and [
\omega
] from
(
3
)
. Although it is
derivable from the other rules, we also explicitly introduce the
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}HH}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

rule from
(
3
)
below, since it is used and referred to in

Section

9
.

y_{0}\cap\texttt{su}=\emptyset

y_{1}\cap\texttt{Pvar}(f,\texttt{o}_{\texttt{Cl}})=\emptyset

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}HH}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\left\langle\texttt{p}+\frac{y_{0}\cdot(y_{1}+\overline{f})}{2},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}\cup y_{0}\cup y_{1}}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\left\langle\texttt{p},{2^{\#y_{0}}}\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}[y_{1}\leftarrow f]

y\notin\texttt{su}

y\notin\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s},\texttt{o}

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}PhaseBisector-PB}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1}+y\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}\cup\{y\}}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\left\langle\texttt{p}+\frac{\texttt{p}_{2}}{2},(2\operatorname{\texttt{cos}}\pi\texttt{p}_{2})\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Filter}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\left\langle\texttt{p},(\Pi y)\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}\cup y}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}[y\leftarrow 1]

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}({\{0,1\}}^{\#(y)})
bijective

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}CV}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}[y\leftarrow\sigma(y)]

World-Relative Phase Equivalence.

The generalized global phase elimination rule introduces a modulo-phase
\equiv

between
HPS
equivalence: a phase factor that is uniform across each given
world of a
HPS
is physically undetectable. We write
\texttt{p}_{2}\in\mathbb{D}[c\mid c\in\lceil\texttt{Cl}\rceil]
to indicate that
\texttt{p}_{2}
is a polynomial over the dyadics
in the boolean expressions appearing in the classical memory.

\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1}+\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}

\texttt{p}_{2}\in\mathbb{D}[c\mid c\in\lceil\texttt{Cl}\rceil]

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Phase-Elimination-PE}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}\equiv\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}

Local Reasoning.

We may also perform rewriting locally after a case analysis or a subspace
analysis. For this, we introduce the case analysis rule
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and the
subspace factorization rule
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}FD}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, as well as compatibility rules for
these decompositions (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Plus}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Times}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
). To these is added a
whole set of algebraic properties of
\psadd
and
\otimes
replicating the
structure of the underlying vector space (e.g. commutativity, distributivity,
etc.)

\texttt{Pvar}(\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{o}_{1},\texttt{s}_{1},\texttt{su}_{1})\cap\texttt{Pvar}(\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{o}_{2},\texttt{s}_{2},\texttt{su}_{2})=\emptyset

\texttt{Oad}_{1}\cap\texttt{Oad}_{2}=\emptyset

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Factor./Distr.-FD}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{s}_{1}\cdot\texttt{o}_{1}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{1}}\pstens\left\langle\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}_{2}\cdot\texttt{o}_{2}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{2}}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1}+\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}_{1}*\texttt{s}_{2}\cdot\texttt{o}_{1}\cup\texttt{o}_{2}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{1}\cup\texttt{su}_{2}}

y\in\texttt{Pvar}(\texttt{h})

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}[y:=0]\psadd\texttt{h}[y:=1]

\texttt{h}_{1}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Plus}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}_{1}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2}

\texttt{h}_{1}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Times}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}\otimes\texttt{h}_{1}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}\otimes\texttt{h}_{2}

Discarding and
HPS
Refinements.

When the state of a composite hybrid system
AB
is separable (not entangled), the

HPS
representing it can be factored as
\texttt{h}_{AB}=\texttt{h}_{A}\otimes\texttt{h}_{B}
using
(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}FD}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
). Under such conditions, it is meaningful to
discard
a
subsystem (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Disc.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) and obtain a partial description of the state
instead. We obtain a preorder relation which we call
HPS
refinement

and denote
\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}
.

\left|\texttt{h}_{1}\right|=1

\texttt{h}_{1}\cap X=\emptyset

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Disc.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{2}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}

\texttt{h}_{1}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}

\texttt{h}_{2}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{3}

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}{}-Trans.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}_{1}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{3}

\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}-Comp.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}

\texttt{h}_{1}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}

(
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\otimes-Mon.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)

\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{3}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}\otimes\texttt{h}_{3}

\texttt{h}_{1}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}

\left(\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket-Mon.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}_{1})\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}_{2})

This subsystem discard rule has important implications on formal verification as
it provides a satisfaction relation
\preccurlyeq_{\texttt{r}}
.

Theorem 7.1 (Soundness of
HPS
reductions).

Let
h
and
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
be two
HPS
, then,

•

If
\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})

•

If
\texttt{h}\equiv\texttt{h}^{\prime}
then
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(\texttt{h})=\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})

•

If
\texttt{h}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}
then there is
\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}
with
|\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}|=1
and
h
containing no input
variables
x
.

8.
Reasoning about Hybrid Quantum Programs

8.1.
Forward Symbolic Semantics

A valid
\lambda
-term for obtaining a parametrized hybrid circuit is a term of
type Prog. The typing judgement
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash t:\text{Prog}
may have
validity constraints in
\Delta
. After instantiation of the parameters and
normalization in the
\lambda
-calculus, this term has the form of one of the
constructors of the type
Prog
. We can therefore inductively define the
interpretation of
t
as a transformation of
HPS
; i.e. a function mapping an

HPS
to another. The transformations are given in
Table

4

with the example of the
Z^{*}=\{H,X\}\cup{\{Z_{k}\}}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
set of gates from Section
5
. For sake of
readability, we suppose that
Qu
is a single qubit register with
\texttt{qu}=\texttt{Qu}[0]
. The
general case is easily obtained by iterated parallelism.

In figure
4
, we use notations briefly summarized as:
\texttt{h}+_{\texttt{p}}\texttt{p}
adds
p
to the phase of
h
(for instance,
\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle+_{\texttt{p}}\texttt{p}_{2}=\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1}+\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle
);
\texttt{h}\cup_{\texttt{su}}\{y\}
adds a new
path-variable
y
to the support of
h
;
\texttt{h}[\texttt{qu}\leftarrow\texttt{b}]
replaces
the value of
qu
in
h
by
b
;
\texttt{h}[\texttt{Cl}\leftarrow\texttt{b}]
appends
b
to
the history stack in the register
Cl
; and
\lceil\texttt{qu}\rceil
returns the value of
qu
in

h
.

Soundness of the
HPS
Semantics.

We establish the soundness of our symbolic semantics with respect to the
cq-state formalism and the
qhl-cv
language
(
36
)
,
as it is the closest to a standard among existing propositions for the formalization of programming languages over hybrid data. Technically, modulo some rewriting, the object languages only
differ in that the loops of H
Qbricks
are bounded

for
loops, while those of
qhl-cv
are potentially
unbounded
while
loops.
5
5

5

At the constructor level, an
additional apparent differences comes from the fact
S
language also
provides oracle probabilistic classical assignment
c:=_{\$}p
. This is
covered by H
Qbricks
through the possibility of introducing non-unitary
elementary operations
through
HPS
such as
|0\rangle\mapsto\sqrt{p_{i}}|i\rangle
. See
Section

9.3
for an application to error modelling.

In the following theorem, we write

\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{cq}
for the cq-state semantics
and we identify density operator maps from

Definition

6.4
to cq-states (provided they are obtained from an
HPS

of norm
\leq 1
).

Theorem 8.1 (Soundness of the semantics).

H
Qbricks
is sound with respect to
qhl-cv
: there is a
(non-surjective) mapping
\phi
from H
Qbricks
to language
S
such that for
any closed H
Qbricks
program
\Pr
(i.e. a program where all parameters
have been instantiated) and an
HPS

h
with norm
\leq 1
,

\mathcal{DO}(\llbracket\Pr\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))=\llbracket\phi(\Pr)\rrbracket_{cq}(\mathcal{DO}(\texttt{h}))

Proof sketch:

After defining a mostly cosmetic translation of the programs of H
Qbricks

in the language of
qhl-cv
, the proof proceeds by induction on the
constructors of
Pr
. For each of the constructors, we show that the
symbolic semantics
\llbracket\texttt{Pr}\rrbracket(\texttt{h})
matches the denotational
semantics of
qhl-cv
. The complete induction is developed in
Appendix
A.2
.
∎

8.2.
A Minimal
HPS
Based Assertion Language

HPS
provide a sound symbolic representation for hybrid programs semantics, paving the way for the static analysis of these programs. In the present section we introduce a core assertion language on top of
HPS
derivation. As illustrated in Section
9
, assertions introduced below are sufficient for reaching the standard literature specifications for all application cases we thought about.

Full Hybrid State Description Assertions.

The most straightforward usage of
HPS
is to derive the result
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
of
executing a hybrid program
p
on a symbolic hybrid state
h
. The induced
assertion is written
\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}
and it is tested by the following condition, with soundness established through Theorem
7.1
.

\left\{{\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{h}}\right\}{\text{Prog}}\left\{{\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}}\right\}\textit{ iff }\llbracket\text{Prog}\rrbracket(\texttt{h})\equiv\texttt{h}^{\prime}

Satisfaction Assertions.

Our refinement relation
\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}
, which formalizes the Discard operation,
directly induces a satisfaction relation
\preccurlyeq_{\texttt{r}}
as its logical
counterpart, referring to
HPS
as predicates over hybrid classical/quantum
states. This allows to specify properties of a state without the need to
describe the full state. For example, such properties include specifying values
of classical registers. The satisfaction of this assertion is given by:

\left\{{\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{h}}\right\}{\text{Prog}}\left\{{\preccurlyeq_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}}\right\}\textit{ iff there is $\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}$ such
that $\left|\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}\right|=1$ and }\llbracket\text{Prog}\rrbracket(\texttt{h})\equiv\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}

with soundness established, again, through
Theorem
7.1
. Note that the specification requires an
existential witness. Concretely, the test is built-in and does not require
writing
HPS
variables.

Probabilistic Satisfaction Assertions.

There is an even more general way to specify properties of a state, by
quantifying the probability that a certain quantum or classical property is
true. More formally, given an
HPS

h
, we can quantify the probability of being
in a world of that
HPS
in which a certain property
cond
holds.
The induced assertion is written
\mathbb{P}\left(\texttt{cond}\right)\sim r
and it is
defined as:

\left\{{\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{h}}\right\}{\text{Prog}}\left\{{\mathbb{P}\left(\texttt{cond}\right)\sim r}\right\}\textit{ iff }\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}\eta\in\llbracket\text{Prog}\rrbracket(\texttt{h})\\
\eta\models\texttt{cond}\end{smallmatrix}}\left|\mathcal{V}(\llbracket\text{Prog}\rrbracket\texttt{h})(\eta)\right|\sim r

where
\eta,\texttt{h}\models\texttt{cond}
is evaluated as a standard boolean
formula, quantum atoms
are evaluated under state

(\texttt{h})(\eta)\preccurlyeq_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{At}
, and with
\sim
being
one of
\leq,\geq,=,<,>
.

Discussion.

As illustrated in
Section

9
, this core assertion language is sufficient
for expressing textbook specifications for a broad range of hybrid computing
features. Nevertheless, it does not allow expressing some advanced features such
as program approximations or relational assertions (approximate correctness,
quantitative bounds)
(
86
;
6
;
93
)
. We do not see any fundamental limitations that would impede extending our
approach to such cases; nonetheless, we leave this for future work, as we first
focus on fundamental basic assertions.

and their different features on a representative set of hybrid quantum programs: the Quantum Phase Estimation algorithm — both in the exact and in the approximate case
(

), a bounded version of the Repeat-Until-Success pattern
(

) and a basic quantum error correction scheme
(

). Together with the teleportation running example, these cases illustrate the main rewriting, refinement, and assertion features presented so far.

, refining classification from

over the

) and

Let us first introduce some
H

syntactic sugar:

\centering\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}\texttt{Q}\triangleright\mathcal{G}&:=&\textbf{Apply}\ \mathcal{G}(\texttt{Q})&&\bm{\texttt{b}}\Rightarrow\texttt{Pr}&:=&\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }\textbf{Skip}\textbf{ end}\\

9.1.
Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE)

Developed by Cleve et al.
(
24
)
and
Kitaev
(
55
)
, Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE) is a central
piece in many quantum algorithms, such as quantum
simulation
(
41
)
and the HHL
algorithm
(
46
)
which solves linear systems of equations in
PolyLog time. QPE takes as input a unitary operator
U
and an Eigenvector

|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
of
U
represented as a
HPS

\texttt{h}=\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}_{\texttt{E}}\right\rangle
of norm
1
, and finds the eigenvalue
e^{2i\pi\theta}
associated with
|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
. In other words: assuming the
existence of a
\theta
satisfying
\left\{{\texttt{h}}\right\}{O}\left\{{\texttt{h}+_{\texttt{p}}\theta}\right\}
, the goal
is to find it. More precisely, we search for the value

\tilde{\theta}\in[0;2^{n})
that minimizes the distance
\tilde{\theta}-\frac{\theta}{2^{n}}
(modulo 1). The
target specification is that the QPE output is
\tilde{\theta}
with
probability at least
\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}
. Detailed introduction to the case is
given in
(
66
)
Chapter 5, among others.

Figure
2
presents the symbolic circuit for QPE and its
H
Qbricks
code together with its specification derivation. In the
specifications, the function
\texttt{int}(Y)
refers to the integer interpretation
of bit vectors:
\texttt{int}(Y):=\sum_{j=0}^{2n-1}Y(j)*2^{j}
. QPE goes through
three main steps:

(1)

The state
|0\rangle_{\texttt{C}}|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
is raised to the
superposition
\left(\sum_{0\leq k<2^{n}}|k\rangle_{\texttt{c}}\right)\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
by a parallel application of the Hadamard
gate in register
C
. This is encoded by the
HPS

\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}}}\cdot|Y_{0}\rangle_{\texttt{C}}\right\rangle_{Y_{0}}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
.

(2)

A series of
U^{2^{i}}
gates is applied to
register
E
each controlled by the value in register

c
at position

i^{\text{th}}
, so as to produce the state

\left\langle\frac{\theta\cdot\texttt{int}(Y_{0})}{2^{n}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}}}\cdot|Y_{0}\rangle_{\texttt{C}}\right\rangle_{Y_{0}}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
,

(3)

This last is a close approximation of

|\text{QFT}(\tilde{\theta})\rangle_{\texttt{c}}|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
,
the result of applying the Quantum Fourier Transform, a standard unitary
primitive, to
|\tilde{\theta}\rangle_{\texttt{c}}|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
. Hence,
applying the inverse of QFT,
written QFT
†
,
to
\left\langle\frac{\theta\cdot\texttt{int}(Y_{0})}{2^{n}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}}}\cdot|Y_{0}\rangle_{\texttt{C}}\right\rangle_{Y_{0}}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}

results in an approximation of state

|\tilde{\theta}\rangle_{\texttt{c}}|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
that is good
enough to output value
\lceil\texttt{C}\rceil=\tilde{\theta}
with probability

\geq\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}
after measurement.

With regard to specification, the QPE case is twofold, depending on whether the
sought eigenvalue has (exact case, when
\theta=\tilde{\theta}
) or does not
have (approximate) an exact
n
bits decomposition. In the exact case the
algorithm guarantee is to output the exact eigenvalue with certainty.
The probabilistic specification is obtained by rule
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Filter}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. In the approximate case, the resulting geometric series is computed by use of
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}PhaseBisector}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{ll}\textbf{Let }QPE(U,\texttt{c},\texttt{E},\texttt{C})=\{&\equiv_{\texttt{r}}|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}\\
\hskip 8.19447pt\textbf{Init}\ (\texttt{c});\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}FD}&\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,1\cdot|0\rangle_{\texttt{c}}\right\rangle{1}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}\par\\
\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{c}\triangleright H;&\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}}}\cdot|Y_{0}\rangle_{\texttt{c}}\right\rangle_{Y_{0}}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}\\
\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{c}\rightrightarrows(\texttt{E}\triangleright U^{k+1});&\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\par\left\langle\frac{\theta\cdot\texttt{int}(Y_{0})}{2^{n}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}}}\cdot|Y_{0}\rangle_{\texttt{c}}\right\rangle_{Y_{0}}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}\par\\
\hskip 8.19447ptc\triangleright QFT^{\dagger};&\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle\frac{\texttt{int}(Y_{0})(\theta-\texttt{int}(Y_{1}))}{2^{n}},\frac{1}{2^{n}}\cdot|Y_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{c}}\right\rangle_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}\par\\
\hskip 8.19447ptc\multimap\texttt{C}\}&\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle\frac{\texttt{int}(Y_{0})(\theta-\texttt{int}(Y_{1}))}{2^{n}},\frac{1}{2^{n}}\cdot{[Y_{1}]}_{\texttt{C}}\right\rangle_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}\otimes|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}FD/Disc/Filter/PB}\hfil\lx@intercol&\mathbb{P}\left(\lceil\texttt{C}\rceil=\tilde{\theta}\right)=\left|\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\sum_{y_{0}}e^{2\pi iY_{0}\frac{\theta-\tilde{\theta}}{2^{n}}}\right|^{2}\ \geq\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}\\
