Figure 2
.

Quantum Phase Estimation, code, specification derivation and
circuit

9.2.
Repeat Until Success (RUS)

Repeat-Until-Success (RUS) is a standard routine in quantum programming: given a
stochastic quantum process
U
achieving a success condition
cont
with
probability
\delta
, it consists in repeating iterations of
U
until reaching this
condition. Also referred to as
post-selection
, the scheme essentially enables one to select a desired result from a
non-deterministic process, shifting the indeterminacy from the computation
output to its execution time. It is used at various levels in the quantum
development process, be it as an elementary brick in involved microcode
implementation protocols (magic state distillation
(
58
)
) or as
the highest-level layer for most quantum algorithms with non-deterministic
outcome (Grover, Shor, Phase estimation, etc.).

We develop a parameter-bounded version of the scheme, Bounded Repeat Until
Success (BRUS), where the number of loop calls is bounded by a user-fixed
parameter.

Table 6
.

Bounded Repeat Until Success: code and specifications

\begin{array}[]{c|c}\textit{Code + spec.}&\textit{Invariant definition}\\
\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{lc}\textbf{Let }\texttt{BRUS}(\{U,\texttt{Q}\},\{\texttt{C}\},\texttt{cont},n)=\{&\Gamma_{0}\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{For }i=1,n\textbf{ do }&\Gamma_{i}\\
\hskip 17.00024pt(\neg\texttt{cont}(\lceil\texttt{C}_{c}\rceil))\Rightarrow\ U(\{\texttt{Q}\},\{\texttt{C}\})&\Gamma_{i+1}\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\textbf{done}\}&\Gamma_{n}\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rl}\Gamma{i}(\delta,\texttt{cont},\texttt{C}_{c}):=&\preccurlyeq_{\texttt{r}}\texttt{h},\\
&\mathbb{P}\left(\texttt{cont}(\lceil\texttt{C}_{c}\rceil)\right)\geq 1-{(1-\delta)}^{i},\\
&\mathbb{P}\left(\neg\texttt{cont}(\lceil\texttt{C}_{c}\rceil)\right)\leq{(1-\delta)}^{i},\\
\end{array}\end{array}

The code and specifications are given abstractly in
Table

6
.
They rely on a non-instantiated invariant condition
\Gamma_{i}
(formalized in

Table

6
, right side), such that:

•

For all integers
i
,
\Gamma_{i}
entails

\mathbb{P}\left(\textsf{cont}(\texttt{C}_{c})\right)\geq 1-{(1-\delta)}^{i}
;

•

Any conditional application of
U(\{\texttt{Q}\},\{\texttt{C}\})

from a
\Gamma_{i}
state ends in a

\Gamma_{i+1}
state.

Then, any iterative execution of
n
application leads from a
\Gamma_{0}
state
to a
\Gamma_{n}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}\left(\textsf{cont}(\texttt{C}_{c})\right)\geq 1-{(1-\delta)}^{i}
.
The proof of correctness requires the invariant
\Gamma_{i}
to be of the form
\texttt{h}\otimes(\texttt{h}_{i}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime}_{i})
, that is a tensored composition
of:

•

an invariant component
h
. In the QPE example from
above, it corresponds to the description of the
initial state of quantum register
E
:

|v\rangle_{\texttt{E}}
, and

•

a variant component, itself structured as a path-sum addition

\texttt{h}_{i}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime}_{i}
whose terms are separated by the classically read
condition
\texttt{cont}(\texttt{C}_{c})
.

Then we analyse
\texttt{h}_{i}
and
\texttt{h}^{\prime}_{i}
separately. The
instruction
(\neg\texttt{cont}(\lceil\texttt{C}_{c}\rceil))\Rightarrow\ U(\{\texttt{Q}\},\{\texttt{C}\})
triggers the execution of
U
in the
\texttt{h}_{i}

component but not in
\texttt{h}^{\prime}_{i}
and it transforms
\texttt{h}_{i}
into a path-sum addition
with a
(\left|\texttt{h}_{i}\right|\cdot\delta)
probable component satisfying

\texttt{cont}(\lceil\texttt{C}_{c}\rceil)
. Adequate information discarding then
enables reshaping the induced three-term
path-sum addition into the
\Gamma_{i+1}
form.

We applied this generic loop scheme to both a parametrized iteration of Hadamard gates and our instances of the Phase Estimation circuit from
Section

9.1
. Experimental details are provided in
Table

7
. We think this development as a foundational step toward the formalization of Hoare style
reasoning and probability propagation upon
HPS
analysis tools.

9.3.
Quantum Errors and Quantum Error Correction

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{tabular}[]{ll}\lx@intercol\text{{Let} QEC3({Q,$\texttt{C}$})=\lx@text@lbrace}\hfil\lx@intercol&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,1\cdot|x\rangle_{\psi}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt$\texttt{Q}_{0},\texttt{Q}_{1}:=0$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,1\cdot|x\rangle_{\psi}|0\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|0\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt$\psi\Rightarrow(\texttt{Q}_{0}\triangleright X)$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,1\cdot|x\rangle_{\psi}|x\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|0\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt$\texttt{Q}_{0}\Rightarrow(\texttt{Q}_{1}\triangleright X)$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,1\cdot|x\rangle_{\psi}|x\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|x\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt$\psi,\texttt{Q}_{0},\texttt{Q}_{1}\triangleright\tilde{I}$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}{[w]}_{\bot}\right\rangle_{\{w\}}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt$c_{0},c_{1}:=0$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}|0\rangle_{c_{0}}|0\rangle_{c_{1}}{[w]}_{\bot}\right\rangle_{\{w\}}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt$c_{0},c_{1}\triangleright H$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}|y_{0}\rangle_{c_{0}}|y_{1}\rangle_{c_{1}}{[w]}_{\bot}\right\rangle_{\{w,y_{0},y_{1}\}}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt\par$c_{0}\Rightarrow(\psi\triangleright Z;\texttt{Q}_{0}\triangleright Z)$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle\frac{y_{0}(w_{0}+w_{1})}{2},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{2}}}\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot\begin{array}[]{ll}|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}&|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}\\
|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}&|y_{0}\rangle_{c_{0}}|y_{1}\rangle_{c_{1}}\end{array}\right\rangle_{\{w,y_{0},y_{1}\}}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt\par$c_{1}\Rightarrow(\texttt{Q}_{0}\triangleright Z;\texttt{Q}_{1}\triangleright Z)$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle\frac{y_{0}(w_{0}+w_{1})+y_{1}(w_{1}+w_{2})}{2},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{2}}}\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot\begin{array}[]{ll}|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}&|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}\\
|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}&|y_{0}\rangle_{c_{0}}|y_{1}\rangle_{c_{1}}\end{array}{[w]}_{\bot}\right\rangle_{\{w,y_{0},y_{1}\}}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt\par$c_{0},c_{1}\triangleright H$;&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle\frac{y_{0}(w_{0}+w_{1})+y_{1}(w_{1}+w_{2})+y_{2}y_{0}+y_{3}y_{2}}{2},{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{2}}}\sqrt{f(w)}}\right.$\\
&\hskip 13.6113pt$\left.{\begin{array}[]{ll}|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}&|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}\\
|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}&|y_{2}\rangle_{c_{0}}|y_{3}\rangle_{c_{1}}\end{array}{[w]}_{\bot}}\right\rangle_{\{w,y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}}$\\
\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}HH^{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\hfil\lx@intercol
&$\left\langle 0,\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}|w_{0}+w_{1}\rangle_{c_{0}}|w_{1}+w_{2}\rangle_{c_{1}}{[w]}_{\bot}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt\par$c\multimap$$\texttt{C}$ \lx@text@rbrace&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot|x\oplus w_{0}\rangle_{\psi}|x\oplus w_{1}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|x\oplus w_{2}\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}{[w_{0}+w_{1}]}_{\texttt{C}_{0}}{[w_{1}+w_{2}]}_{\texttt{C}_{1}}{[w]}_{\bot}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}$\\
\hskip 6.80565pt\par\par$\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{C}_{0}\texttt{C}_{1}\Rightarrow(\texttt{Q}_{0}\triangleright X);\\
\texttt{C}_{0}\overline{\texttt{C}_{1}}\Rightarrow(\psi\triangleright X);\\
\overline{\texttt{C}_{0}}\texttt{C}_{1}\Rightarrow(\texttt{Q}_{1}\triangleright X);\end{array}$&$\equiv_{\texttt{r}}\left\langle 0,\sqrt{f(w)}\cdot|x\oplus(\sum_{w}\geq 2)\rangle_{\psi}|x\oplus(\sum_{w}\geq 2)\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{0}}|x\oplus(\sum_{w}\geq 2)\rangle_{\texttt{Q}_{1}}{[w_{0}+w_{1}]}_{\texttt{C}_{0}}{[w_{1}+w_{2}]}_{\texttt{C}_{1}}{[w]}_{\bot}\right\rangle_{\emptyset}$\\
\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}\mathbb{P}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$ $\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}/Disc.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\hfil\lx@intercol&$\mathbb{P}\left(\lceil\psi\texttt{Q}_{0}\texttt{Q}_{1}\rceil=xxx\right)={(1-p)}^{3}+3p{(1-p)}^{2}$\\
\end{tabular}&\hskip-147.95424pt\begin{tabular}[]{c}\scalebox{.76}{

\end{tabular}\par\end{array}

Figure 3
.

Circuit and
HPS
derivation for the 3-qubit error correction code

This example illustrates the possibility to encode non-deterministic processes
(Kraus operators) and error models and correction in
HPS
. Errors can be
modeled as mixed gates
(f,{\{U_{w}\}}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}})
which
act as
U_{w}
with probability
f(w)
. Formally,

\llbracket(f,\{U_{w}\})\rrbracket_{w\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}}(\texttt{h})=\psadd_{w\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}}\sqrt{f(w)}\llbracket U_{w}(\texttt{h})\rrbracket\otimes{[w]}_{\bot}

where the
\bot
register is to be interpreted as an inaccessible ‘dump site’
address, allowing world splitting without access to the values.

For instance, consider the three-qubit code described in details by
Roffe
(
76
)
with its circuit and the corresponding specification derivation in
HPS
given in
Fig.

3
. Here,
an erroneous identity operator
\tilde{I}
, behaves as the identity with
probability
p
and otherwise applies a bit flip. In
Fig.

3
, the state

|x\rangle_{\psi}
is first broadcast to the register
\psi\texttt{Q}_{0}\texttt{Q}_{1}
(Step
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
). Then, each qubit in
\psi\texttt{Q}_{0}\texttt{Q}_{1}
undergoes the
non-deterministic erroneous identity
\tilde{I}
(Step
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) modeling the
occurrence of errors. Next, a
syndrome
is measured by applying

Z
-stabilizers controlled by ancilla (Step
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{3}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) and these ancillas are
measured (Step
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{4}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
). The results are then processed by classical control to
deduce a correction to apply (Step
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{5}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
).

Note that Step
\color[rgb]{0,0.3906,0}\bm{5}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
performs successive control on the same measurement results.
Therefore, storing that classical data in actual memory cells is unavoidable for this
case, a requirement that is missed by most pre-existing solutions (see
Table

1
).

10.
Implementation and Experiments

We have implemented H
Qbricks
and the
HPS
framework described so far in OCaml.
This development provides the full material introduced so far
and enables symbolic execution over hybrid quantum programs for our core assertion language.
It consists of 14567 lines of code, including
H
Qbricks
and a
HPS
library, tests that cover both the
implementation and analysis of the illustrative cases from
Section

9
, an
intermediate representation with translations to and from OpenQASM2, and a
Jupyter notebook tutorial providing an accessible and interactive introduction
to the tool.

Our experiments cover the standard hybrid quantum use
cases from the literature from
Table

5
. Different implementation metrics are reported in
Table

7
:
number of (quantum + classical)
wires
, number of quantum

gates
, number of alternative measurement scenarios (
worlds
)
computation time
in seconds
and number of
Rewriting rules
automatically triggered.
In addition to the final specifications to verify (assertions from the language
in
Section

8.2
), our experiments require the user to provide a generic invariant for the BRUS scheme case. Note that this only
concerns the generic scheme: no additional input is required for applying BRUS
to a specific unitary function. Other cases are fully automated. The last column in
Table

7
provides data about automatically triggered rewriting rules. For example, the
quantum teleportation with
50\ 000
wires automatically triggers
10\ 000

occurrences of the ChangeVariable (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}CV}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) rule and one occurrence of both the
Factorize-Distr (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}FD}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) and the Discard (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Disc.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) rules.

Table

7
also provides, when available, computation time data for similar tests from the literature, along with the related references.

System Configuration.
All the following experiments were conducted on a Dell
laptop equipped with an Intel Core i7-12800H CPU (14 cores, 20 threads, up to
4.8\text{\,}\mathrm{G}\mathrm{H}\mathrm{z}
),
64\text{\,}\mathrm{G}\mathrm{B}
DDR5 RAM, and an NVIDIA T600 Laptop GPU, running Ubuntu 24.04.2
LTS with Linux kernel 6.14.

The
Quantum Teleportation
experiment is the parameterized
version of our running example protocol, as described in

Section

4
, where Alice sends an entire quantum register of
varying size to Bob, instead of a single qubit. As reported in

Table

7
, H
Qbricks
scales up to
10000
teleported qubits in

\simeq 30
seconds (the total circuit requires
3
quantum wires and
2

classical wires per teleported qubit, as appears in
Fig.

1
).
We are not aware of any other experiment of symbolic execution for
teleportation at such a scale. Still, symQV
(
7
)
and
Tensored Decision Diagrams from
(
49
)
provide experiments
for the teleportation of a single qubit (using 5 wires). H
Qbricks
outperforms them
by
6
and
3
orders of magnitude, respectively.

The
Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE)
circuit
is unitary. However, our
experiments integrates measuring this circuit and deriving its textbook
specifications in terms of the probability of obtaining a desired classical result
from this measurement. QPE contains a call to an oracle family
U(k)
. In our
implementation, we used a parametric phase encoding circuit family
U_{\texttt{p}}
.
It is built by parallel occurrences of
Z_{k}
rotations and performs the state
transformation
|j\rangle\rightarrow e^{\frac{k*2i\pi j}{2^{n}}}|j\rangle
.
Hence, any basis state
|j\rangle
such that
0\leq j<2^{n}
,
|j\rangle
is an
eigenstate of
U(k)
with corresponding eigenvalue
e^{\frac{k*2i\pi j}{2^{n}}}
.
Our experiments are led both in the
exact and the approximate case. In the
exact
case, we
used symbolic inputs, so that the verification holds for any
0\leq j<2^{n}
, just as observed above. To illustrate a concrete case and showcase our scaling, we also performed the computation over
the RSA-260 secret-key challenge of the RSA Security LLC lab
(
50
)
(with unknown factor decomposition at present time).

In previous attempt, symQV
(
7
)
provided two instances (with respectively
7
and
11
wires, using the same oracle as us)
6
6

6

in
(
7
;
15
;
44
)
, QPE instances are indexed after the cardinal of the top computing register (register
C
from
Fig.

2
) whereas we index them after the whole number of quantum and classical registers. Therefore, our
7
,
11
,
101
, and
1036
instances are respectively designated as QPE
-3
and QPE
-5
in
(
7
)
, QPE
-50
in
(
15
)
, and QPE
-1024
in
(
44
)
.
of QPE in the approximate case. As shown in
Table

7
, H
Qbricks
performs better by at least
5
orders of magnitude.
Another occurrence, also using the same oracle
7
7

7

More precisely, experiments from
(
7
;
15
)
also make use of the phase shift oracle family, but it is not implemented from elementary gates in the circuit. Instead, they provide the full oracles as elementary gate constructs, together with their functional interpretation. For sake of similarity, in our instances comparing with
(
7
;
15
)
, we used the same abstractions.
, comes from QCEC
(
15
)
and the case with
50
qubits and concrete entries. The reported performance is largely outperformed by H
Qbricks
. In a recent proposition dedicated to the formal verification of QPE
(
44
)
, authors exhibits cases, still using the same oracle, with up to
1024
qubits in the bottom register (
phase qubits
) and
6
qubits in the top register (
precision qubits
). H
Qbricks
runs faster by almost
5
orders of magnitude.

In addition to the results reported in
Table

7
, we performed a
robustness test for QPE calculation: it consists of iterating
100\ 000
runs,
with randomly chosen entries of 100 qubits and a correctness verification
check. The whole test lasted
44
minutes with no reported failure.
Note also that, interestingly, our tool performs with similar times
for the exact and for the approximate case.

The
Bounded Repeat Until Success
is also parametrized by a unitary
subprocess. We led our experiments on both the
parallel Hadamard circuit

and instances of the Phase Estimation introduced above.

As an example, the Phase Estimation case with
300
wires seeks the best
90
bits approximation of a
120
phase qubits eigenvalue. H
Qbricks
verifies, in 0.534 seconds, that the process would succeed
with probability
>0.9945
after
10
tries.
While Repeat-Until-Success (also often called
post-selection
) is a fundamental pattern from quantum computing, the present result is the first attempt to validate it symbolically.

Symbolic execution of
quantum error correction

(
34
;
51
)
and fault-tolerance (i.e., that errors propagate controllably)
(
20
)
was recently addressed.
These works
are
specialized to the modeling of error correction, featuring various correction
schemes and state injection.

What our experiments highlight, however, is the flexibility of our approach,
which is able to address this class of problem without any extension.
For instance, while the codes are different, making the comparison not fully fair, we verified the 3-qubit code with
100
logical qubits, each one requiring
5
quantum wires and

2
classical wires in
0.101
seconds, while, e.g., experiments from
(
51
)

compute a 16-wire-large case featuring the
[[6,1,3]]
stabilizer code in

0.252\text{\,}\mathrm{s}
. These results establish our approach as a promising candidate
for the symbolic verification of error correction at the FTQC scale.

Unitary circuit equivalence: the Quantum Fourier Transform.

As a side effect, our implementation also improve the analysis for the restricted unitary case.
It is a well-known fact that, for unitaries, the equivalence problem (checking whether two circuits
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are functionally equivalent) reduces to a case of specifications verification (namely, whether the sequence
C_{1};C^{\dagger}_{2}
behaves as
Skip
). To illustrate the performance of H
Qbricks
in this setting, we conducted equivalence checking experiments between the standard version of the Quantum Fourier Transform and variants obtained by shuffling gates that commute and adding Clifford gadgets (combinations of
X,H
and
Z
gates) with a neutral net effect.
Table

7
reports these experiments for input sizes varying from
16
to
500
qubits, and compares them with similar experiments from the state of the art.
H
Qbricks
performs better than any found comparative experiments by a factor of, at least
\frac{0.04}{0.024697}\simeq 1.6
(QCEC for the 32 qubits case), and orders of magnitude in most cases.

Conclusion.

H
Qbricks
performs very well for a wide range of standard patterns from quantum computing,
including communication protocols,
post-selection, gate-to-gate circuit composition, and quantum error correction.

11.
Related Work

In the last decade, several efforts have aimed to bring formal methods to quantum
programming
(
18
;
63
;
47
;
95
;
61
)
.
We extend prior work on path-sum analyses
(
18
;
88
;
3
;
4
;
29
)
to the hybrid case.
This allows tractable semantics and specifications, avoiding heavy
formalized density operators while providing flexibility. Flexibility is both
horizontal — the solution supports both quantum and classical
computation, and vertical — both low-/high-level programming
are handled through generic constructors such as the quantum control and gate-to-gate circuit composition.

Our overall proposal gets inspiration from different ingredients that have been
developed separately in the literature.
First are
path-sums
(
3
;
88
)
(for the unitary parts),
including unbalanced
(
4
;
29
)
or
parameterized
(
18
;
29
)
versions. As an extension
of path-sums, the closest work is certainly
(
88
)
. With regard
to this development, our main contributions are the derivation of hybrid
path-sums from H
Qbricks
quantum programs, the formalization of both quantum
and classical control, and the extension of rewriting and simplification tools
with local reasoning facilities.
Additional inspirations for this work include hybrid state modeling
(
36
;
87
)
for the non-unitary parts and quantum Hoare
logic
(
63
;
92
;
85
)
for
specification.

An alternative line of research considers
the equivalence checking problem for instantiated
quantum circuits. It benefits
from a variety of symbolic representations
(automata
(
22
;
21
)
,
path-sums
(
3
)
, binary decision
graphs
(
82
;
83
)
, etc.) and/or verification
techniques (symbolic execution
(
14
;
7
)
, model
checking
(
39
;
39
)
,
assisted proofs
(
95
;
63
)
, etc).

Concerning the analysis of hybrid classical/quantum computing, several propositions
(
14
;
49
;
75
)
take profit on the
deferred measurement principle
. An important bottleneck of this approach is its intrinsic limitation to hybrid circuits with ‘unitary behavior’ in the sense that discarding non important parts of the memory is forbidden. This severely limiting aspect was identified and provided partial solutions in
(
49
;
75
)
.

A last line of research considers fully automatic verification of
simpler properties of quantum programs, such as the validity requirements
pointed out in
Section
5

(
72
;
73
;
38
)
,
or state abstractions
(
94
;
70
;
11
)
. These works also include state-dependent properties, such
as the management (allocation and freeing) of ancillae, with automated
guaranteed synthesis of uncomputation
subroutines
(
10
;
67
;
68
;
78
)
.

12.
Conclusion

In this paper, we introduce the
HPS
formalism, a framework for the verification of
hybrid quantum programs that includes a compact symbolic representation, formal reasoning
capabilities, a core assertion language and a symbolic execution mechanism. This framework has
been implemented into a working prototype, and we demonstrated its relevance through a diverse
panel of typical hybrid case studies, ranging from quantum communication to computing and error
correction.

A current limitation of this work is that the control structures are limited to bounded loops.
However, since
HPS
contain all the necessary information for characterizing their associated multiworld structure in terms of measurable spaces, we plan to generalize their usage to unbounded
loops. Other directions for future work include investigating the (possible extension towards)
completeness of our HPS equational theory, extension to parametrized hybrid quantum programs
and potential integration of SMT solvers.

The reproduction package is available on Zenodo at
https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.19080601
(for the evaluated version, see
(
65
)
). For reuse, the project is available on GitHub at
https://github.com/Qbricks/hqbricks
.

Research Agency (ANR)
within the
framework of “Plan France 2030”, under the research projects EPIQ ANR-22-
PETQ-0007 and HQI-R&D ANR-22-PNCQ-0002.

Supplementary material for the article

“Hybrid Path-Sums for Hybrid Quantum Programs”

Appendix A
Proofs and Supplementary Theorems

A.1.
Supplementary Material Helpful for the Proofs

Theorem A.1 (Conservation of probabilities).

For any program
p
,
HPS

h
, and history
\eta
, we have

|\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)|^{2}=\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\geq\eta}|\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))(\eta^{\prime})|^{2}

In other words, the probability of being in a future of
\eta
after performing the program
p
is the same as the probability of having been in
\eta
before performing
p
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
p
. We elaborate on the case when
p
is a

QProg
. In such a case,
\llbracket p\rrbracket
has the effect of a
unitary operation on
h
possibly parametrized by classical variables (e.g.
classical/hybrid conditioning) and does not change any classical memory;
therefore,
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})
and
\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))
have the same
supports of histories of uniform length
l
, so that if
\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})(\eta^{\prime}))\neq 0
and
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)\neq 0
and
\eta^{\prime}\geq\eta
, then,
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
. On the other hand, we have
\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))(\eta)=U_{\eta}\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)
. Therefore,
\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\geq\eta}|\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))(\eta^{\prime})|^{2}=|\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))(\eta^{\prime})|^{2}=|\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)|^{2}
.

Note: up to this theorem, the set
\mathcal{G}
of operators had no explicit reason to be
limited to unitaries; this theorem is what imposes unitarity.

We will not elaborate fully on all the constructs of programs, but we note
that the proof relies heavily on the properties of the temporal order

\leq
, the
L^{2}
norm, the Born rule, and the structure of
\psadd
with
respect to
\mathcal{V}
. Notably, the cases of sequential composition and

For
require the transitivity of
\leq
. Classical assignment does
not change quantum states, but pushes the histories forward one step in
time, and the equality in the case of measurement is justified by the
coherence of the Born rule with the
L^{2}
justifies the equality. Finally,
for
If
s with classical conditions, we rely heavily on the fact that

b
being classical guarantees that
\mathcal{V}(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})
and

\mathcal{V}(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})
have disjoint supports of histories, so that the

\psadd
operation in
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})\psadd\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})
corresponds to a disjoint union, and does not allow
different worlds to interfere.
∎

The conservation of probabilities is a generalization of unitarity for hybrid
programs, and closely parallels the preservation of trace in the language of
density operators.

Corollary 0 (Conservation of histories).

For any
HPS

h
and any program
p
, if
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})
is supported on
S=\{\eta|\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)\neq 0\}
, then
\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))
is supported exclusively on futures of
S
; that is, if
\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))(\eta)\neq 0
, then
\eta
is the future of some
\eta^{\prime}\in S
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})
and
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
have disjoint support of
histories of the same length
l
, and
p
and
q
are any two programs,

\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))
and
\mathcal{V}(\llbracket q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

also have disjoint supports, and
\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime}
refers purely to the union
part of the operator
\psadd
.

Definition 0 (History and time).

A history
\eta
of length/age
l\in\mathbb{N}
(denoted
|\eta|=l
) is a
stack of length
l
of address-indexed tuples of booleans all of the same
length
l
. A history represents the values taken by the classical memory
over
l
time-steps. As stacks, histories are subject to prefix functions

\pi_{l^{\prime}}
and suffix functions
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}
. When
l^{\prime}\leq|\eta|
, the

\pi_{l^{\prime}}(\eta)
and
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}(\eta)
are the prefix and suffix of
\eta

of length
l^{\prime}
respectively. When
l^{\prime}>|\eta|
,
\pi_{l^{\prime}}(\eta)=\eta\mathbin{+\mkern-5.0mu+}{\sigma_{1}(\eta)}^{(l^{\prime}-|\eta|)}
and
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}(\eta)={\pi_{1}(\eta)}^{(l^{\prime}-|\eta|)}\mathbin{+\mkern-5.0mu+}\eta
where
\mathbin{+\mkern-5.0mu+}
is
concatenation and
\eta^{n}
is
\mathbin{+\mkern-5.0mu+}_{i}^{n}(\eta)
. These three
operations
|\cdot|
,
\pi
, and

\sigma
can be extended to
HPS
by applying to their symbolic
histories in their classical output.
f(\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{o}_{\text{Cl}}\texttt{o}_{\text{Qu}}\cdot\texttt{s}\right\rangle)=\left\langle\texttt{p},f(\texttt{o}_{\text{Cl}})\texttt{o}_{\text{Qu}}\cdot\texttt{s}\right\rangle
for
f\in\{\pi,\sigma\}
and
\text{age}(\texttt{h})=|\texttt{o}_{\text{Cl}}|
which is uniform
across all paths. We then get three orders on histories/
HPS
. We give them
for histories, and they are defined exactly the same for
HPS
.

\begin{array}[]{r r c c l}\text{Age}:&\text{young / old}:&\eta\leq_{a}\eta^{\prime}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&|\eta|\leq|\eta^{\prime}|\\
\text{Time}:&\text{past / future}:&\eta\leq_{\pi}\eta^{\prime}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&|\eta|\leq|\eta^{\prime}|\land\pi_{|\eta|}(\eta^{\prime})=\eta\\
\text{Memory}:&\text{veiling / awakening}:&\eta\leq_{\sigma}\eta^{\prime}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&|\eta|\leq|\eta^{\prime}|\land\sigma_{|\eta|}(\eta^{\prime})=\eta\end{array}

Proof.

(Sketch)

The construction of
\phi
is by induction on the constructors of the type

Prog
, after normalization of the terms according to the rules of the
simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus introduced. In this proof sketch, we select only
a sample of the more interesting translation cases from H
Qbricks
to

qhl-cv
to display.

•

If
p:\text{QProg}
,
p
represents a unitary, so we can define
\phi(p)=(\bar{q}:=U)
.

•

When the set of programs is quotiented over the monoid axioms
corresponding to the sequencing operator
;
and its identity
Skip
0– that is associativity of
;
and identity of
Skip
with respect to
;
0–
\phi
is a morphism of monoids; that is,
\phi(p;q)=\phi(p);\phi(q)
and
\phi(\textbf{Skip})=\textbf{Skip}
.

•

A closed
For
term has its bounds instantiated; therefore, it
can be translated directly as a sequencing of its terms:
\phi(\textbf{For }x_{n}^{I}=i,j\textbf{ do }p\textbf{ done})=\phi(p[x_{n}^{I}:=i];\ldots;p[x_{n}^{I}:=j])
. In terms of the quotient through the monoid rules above,
this expresses that
\textbf{For }x_{n}^{I}=i,j\textbf{ do }p\textbf{ done}
is definable in the monoid as
\prod_{x_{n}^{I}=i}^{j}p
.

The reason such quotienting can be meaningfully used is that the interpretation of
programs
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
remains well-defined modulo this quotient.
With these definitions laid down, the proof of
\llbracket\phi(p)\rrbracket(\phi_{\llbracket\rrbracket}(\texttt{h}))=\phi_{\llbracket\rrbracket}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))
is a relatively
automatic induction on
p
.

∎

Lemma 0 (Linearity and consistency of
\mathcal{V}\circ\llbracket p\rrbracket
).

We have
\forall\texttt{Pr},\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\implies\mathcal{V}(\llbracket\texttt{Pr}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))=\mathcal{V}(\llbracket\texttt{Pr}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
and
(\mathcal{V}\circ\llbracket p\rrbracket)(\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime})=(\mathcal{V}\circ\llbracket p\rrbracket)(\texttt{h})+(\mathcal{V}\circ\llbracket p\rrbracket)(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
. Furthermore, we have
\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}\implies\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})

Proof.

Any program
p
has an associated
HPS

\texttt{h}_{p}
with one free variable of
type
HPS
such that,
\forall\texttt{h},\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\texttt{h}_{p}[\texttt{h}]
.
This can be seen immediately from the definition of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
. The immediate consequence is that
\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}\implies\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
.

Furthermore, in the proof of soundness of the Rewrite rule, we also proved a
slightly different property that
\llbracket p\rrbracket
respects equality
modulo
\mathcal{V}
:
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\implies\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))=\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime}))
. Linearity of
\mathcal{V}\circ\llbracket p\rrbracket
can be proven by induction on
p
where, for the case of
sequencing, we have used that
\llbracket p\rrbracket
respects equality
modulo
\mathcal{V}
and the inductive hypotheses. We note that linearity is trivial
for
Skip
and unitaries.

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p;q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket q\rrbracket(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket q\rrbracket(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})\psadd\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p;q\rrbracket(\texttt{h})+\llbracket p;q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}\psadd\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}))\psadd\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}\psadd\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}\psadd\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime})))+\mathcal{V}(\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}\psadd\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}))+\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}))+\mathcal{V}(\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})+\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})\psadd\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})+\mathcal{V}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime})\psadd\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}))

Finally, both measurement and classical assignment are covered by the
HPS
form

\texttt{h}[c\leftarrow b]
where
c
is a classical register. Recall that
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}[c\leftarrow b])
is null on any history that does not have
b
on the top of the
stack of
c
, so we will only consider the situations where it is.

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}((\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime})[c\leftarrow b])(\eta,c\leftarrow b)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}((\texttt{h}\psadd\texttt{h}^{\prime}))(\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)+\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})(\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}[c\leftarrow b])(\eta,c\leftarrow b)+\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime}[c\leftarrow b])(\eta,c\leftarrow b)

∎

A.2.
Relative Soundness of the Language

We wish to show that our H
Qbricks
language and its
HPS
representation is
sound with respect to cq-states introduced in
(
36
)

A cq-state
\Delta
over a set of quantum variables
V
is a function
\Sigma\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{V})
, where
\Sigma
is the set of classical states
(an assignment of each classical variable to a boolean or integer value) and

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{V})
is the set of partial density operators on (the
Hilbert space generated by)
V
.
Furthermore, to be a cq-state
\Delta
needs to verify the following conditions:

•

its support is countable

•

\textit{trace}(\Delta):=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\Delta}\textit{trace}(\Delta(\sigma))\leq 1

That is for each possible value of the classical states, a (partial) density
operator is given such that the sum of their traces is at most 1.

The language of H
Qbricks
is not the same as the language used in
(
36
)
,
and states in H
Qbricks
are
HPS
while in
(
36
)
, they are cq-states. In
order to compare the two, we need to define a translation of languages

H\textsc{Qbricks}\to\textsc{qhl-cv}
as well as a translation of states
\text{HPS}\to(\Sigma\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{V}))
.

Theorem A.5 (Relative soundness of the language).

H
Qbricks
is sound with respect to
qhl-cv
: there is a mapping

\phi_{\llbracket\rrbracket}
of
HPS
into generalized cq-states, whose
restriction to
HPS
with norm lesser or equal to
1
lends into cq-states, and a
mapping
\phi
of closed H
Qbricks

Prog
terms into
qhl-cv
programs
such that

\llbracket\phi(p)\rrbracket(\phi_{\llbracket\rrbracket}(\texttt{h}))=\phi_{\llbracket\rrbracket}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

Remark 1.

\phi
is not surjective. For example, H
Qbricks
explicitly excludes
unbounded loops, so that some
qhl-cv

While
programs are
not images of any H
Qbricks
program. More specifically, the image of
\phi

includes bounded while loops via
k:=i;\textbf{while }(\bm{\texttt{b}}\land k\leq j)\textbf{ do }p;k:=k+1\textbf{ done}\equiv p[k:=i];\ldots;p[k:=j]
, and excludes general while loops, and the oracles of probabilistic
classical assignment. However, the language of H
Qbricks
is open to
extension by arbitrary unitaries. If we add unitaries corresponding to
probability distributions
p
such that
U_{p}:|0\rangle\mapsto\sqrt{p_{i}}|i\rangle
, probabilistic classical assignment
c:=_{\$}p
is also
possible through the H
Qbricks
program
\textbf{Reset}(q);U_{p}(q);\textbf{Measure}(q,c)
. In essence, while
\phi
is not surjective,
it effectively only misses, intentionally,
unbounded while loops, so the language of H
Qbricks
can be seen as an
extension of that of
qhl-cv
with the restriction of bounded loops.

Proof.

of theorem
8.1

For a closed
HPS

h
,
\phi_{\llbracket\rrbracket}(\texttt{h})=\rho(\texttt{h})
. For programs, we have a translation of QProg terms into unitaries as follows:

\displaystyle U(\textbf{Skip})

\displaystyle=I

\displaystyle U(U(q))

\displaystyle=I_{m}\otimes U\otimes I_{n}

\displaystyle U(p;q)

\displaystyle=U(q)U(p)

\displaystyle U(\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end})

\displaystyle=U(p)P_{\bm{\texttt{b}}}+U(q)P_{\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}}

where
P_{\bm{\texttt{b}}}
is the matrix defined for computational basis states
|\psi\rangle
by
P_{\bm{\texttt{b}}}|\psi\rangle=\psi
if
|\psi\rangle
satisfies
b
given the state of the classical registers and
P_{\bm{\texttt{b}}}|\psi\rangle=0
otherwise. For a
closed
term of type Prog; that is, a program with all parameters specified,

\displaystyle\phi(\textbf{Measure}(q,c))

\displaystyle=c:=\textbf{ meas }\mathcal{M}[q]\quad

\displaystyle\phi(c_{1}:=f(\lceil c_{2}\rceil))

\displaystyle=c_{1}:=c_{2}

\displaystyle\phi(p;q)

\displaystyle=\phi(p);\phi(q)

\displaystyle\phi(\textbf{For }x^{I}_{n}=i,j\textbf{ do }p\textbf{ done})

\displaystyle=

\phi(p[x_{n}^{I}:=i]);\phi(p[x_{n}^{I}:=i+1]);\cdots;\phi(p[x_{n}^{I}:=j])

\displaystyle\phi(\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end})

\displaystyle=\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }\phi(p)\textbf{ else }\phi(q)\textbf{ end}

\displaystyle\text{ when }\bm{\texttt{b}}:\text{CBool}

\displaystyle\phi(p)

\displaystyle=\bar{q}*=U(p)

\displaystyle\text{ when }p:QProg\text{ and }\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}(p)\subseteq q

\phi
respects the monoid axioms for
;
and
Skip
. Explicitly,
\phi(\textbf{Skip})=\textbf{Skip}
and
\phi(p;q)=\phi(p);\phi(q)

by definition.

The proof of the theorem follows by induction on program terms. We give the proof in full detail below.

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(\textbf{Skip})\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{Skip}\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\texttt{h})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket\textbf{Skip}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(p;q)\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(p);\phi(q)\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(q)\rrbracket(\llbracket\phi(p)\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket q\rrbracket(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket p;q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(c_{1}:=f(\lceil c_{2}\rceil))\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket c_{1}:=f(c_{2})\rrbracket(\bigoplus\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|w\rangle\langle w|\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bigoplus\langle c[c(f(c_{2}))/c_{1}],\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|w\rangle\langle w|\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\texttt{h}[c_{1}\leftarrow\lceil f(c_{2})\rceil])

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(\textbf{Measure }(q,c))\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket c:=\textbf{ meas }\mathcal{M}[q]\rrbracket(\bigoplus\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|w\rangle\langle w|\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\bigoplus\langle c[i/x],\sum_{w,c(w)=c}M_{i}|w\rangle\langle w|M_{i}^{\dagger}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\texttt{h}[q\leftarrow c])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket\textbf{Measure }(q,c)\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

If
\bm{\texttt{b}}:\text{CBool}
,

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end})\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }\phi(p)\textbf{ else }\phi(q)\textbf{ end})\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }\phi(p)\textbf{ else }\phi(q)\textbf{ end})\rrbracket(\bigoplus_{c}\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|w\rangle\langle w|\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(p)\rrbracket(\bigoplus_{c,c\models\bm{\texttt{b}}}\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|w\rangle\langle w|\rangle)+\llbracket\phi(q)\rrbracket(\bigoplus_{c,c\models\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}}\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|w\rangle\langle w|\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(p)\rrbracket(\phi(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}))+\llbracket\phi(q)\rrbracket(\phi(\bar{\bm{\texttt{b}}}\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h}))+\phi(\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{\bm{\texttt{b}}}\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\texttt{b}}\texttt{h})\psadd\llbracket q\rrbracket(\bar{\bm{\texttt{b}}}\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

If
p:\text{QProg}
,

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(p)\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\llbracket U(p)\rrbracket(\bigoplus_{c}\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|w\rangle\langle w|\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bigoplus_{c}\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}U(p)|w\rangle\langle w|{U(p)}^{\dagger}\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bigoplus_{c}\langle c,\sum_{w,c(w)=c}|U(p)w\rangle\langle U(p)w|\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi(\textbf{For }x_{n}^{I}=i,j\textbf{ do }p\textbf{ done})\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=\llbracket\phi(p[x^{I}_{n}:=i]);\phi(p[x^{I}_{n}:=i+1];\ldots;\phi([x^{I}_{n}:=j])\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=\llbracket\phi(p[x^{I}_{n}:=j])\rrbracket\circ\cdots\circ\llbracket\phi(p[x^{I}_{n}:=i]\rrbracket(\phi(\texttt{h}))

\displaystyle=\llbracket\phi(p[x^{I}_{n}:=j])\rrbracket\circ\cdots\circ\llbracket p[x^{I}_{n}:=i+1]\rrbracket(\phi(\llbracket p[x^{I}_{n}:=i]\rrbracket(\texttt{h})))

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=\phi(\llbracket p[x^{I}_{n}:=j]\rrbracket\circ\cdots\circ\llbracket p[x^{I}_{n}:=i]\rrbracket(\texttt{h})))

\displaystyle=\phi(\llbracket p[x^{I}_{n}:=i];\ldots;p[x^{I}_{n}:=j]\rrbracket(\texttt{h})))

\displaystyle=\phi(\llbracket\textbf{For }x_{n}^{I}=i,j\textbf{ do }p\textbf{ done}\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))

∎

A.3.
Equational Theory

Theorem A.6 (Soundness of
HPS
reductions).

For any closed
HPS

h
and
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
,

•

If
\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})

•

If
\texttt{h}\equiv\texttt{h}^{\prime}
then
\tilde{}\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})=\tilde{}\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})

Proof.

of Theorem
7.1

y\in\texttt{su}(\texttt{h})

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}[y:=0]\psadd\texttt{h}[y:=1]

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Scalar}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\left\langle\texttt{p},\alpha\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\alpha\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Phase}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\left\langle\theta+\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}e^{2\pi i\theta}\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle

The soundness of these rules follows immediately once
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})
is expanded:

\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)=\sum_{y_{0},\text{history}(\texttt{h}[y:=y_{0}])=\eta}\texttt{s}(y_{0})\cdot e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}(y_{0})}|\texttt{o}_{\text{qu}}\rangle

The Scalar and Phase rules express the factorization of constants out of the
sum, and Split expresses the decomposition of the sum as first summing over
y\in y
, then over
y\setminus\{y\}
.

The properties of
\mathcal{V}
being linear and respecting
\otimes
imply the
soundness of the axioms of
HPS
forming a
c
-vector space modulo
\equiv^{\texttt{p}}

and
\otimes
being commutative, associative, and bilinear with respect to

\psadd
modulo
\equiv^{\texttt{p}}
. In fact, these axioms modulo
\equiv^{\texttt{p}}
are nothing
more than the pullbacks of the respective axioms for
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})
. The soundness of
the
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Plus}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Times}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
rules also follow naturally from that.

We note also that the
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Fact-Distr}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
rule is
correct by definition: the left-hand side of
\equiv^{\texttt{p}}
is
defined
as
the right-hand side.

We introduce another rule derived from
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, namely,

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split-Many}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, which is useful for the remainder of the proof.

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split-Many}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
consists simply of repeating Split to many path variables;
its use lies in the brevity it provides.

y\subseteq\texttt{su}(\texttt{h})

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split-Many}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
—
\#(y)

\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\psadd_{t\in 2^{\#(y)}}\texttt{h}[y:=t]

The remaining
\equiv^{\texttt{p}}
rules are proven sound by their construction using the
given rules. We give the details of these constructions below.

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Filter}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p},\left(\prod y\right)\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}\cup y}

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split-Many}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\psadd_{y_{0}\in 2^{|y|}\setminus\{1\}}(\left\langle\texttt{p},0\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}})\psadd\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Scalar}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}0\cdot\psadd_{y_{0}\in 2^{|y|}\setminus\{1\}}(\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}})\psadd\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbb{C}\text{-vector space}}\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Change-Var}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
:

h

\displaystyle\equiv_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split-Many}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\psadd_{y_{0}\in 2^{|y|}}\texttt{h}[y:=y_{0}]

\displaystyle\equiv_{\mathbb{C}\text{-vector space}}\psadd_{y_{0}\in 2^{|y|}}\texttt{h}[y:=\sigma(y_{0})]

\displaystyle=\psadd_{y_{0}\in 2^{|y|}}\texttt{h}[y:=\sigma(y)][y:=y_{0}]

\displaystyle\equiv_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split-Many}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\texttt{h}[y:=\sigma(y)]

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Phase-Bisector}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}+y\overline{Q},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Split-Many}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\psadd_{y^{\prime}_{0}=\texttt{su}}(\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle\psadd\left\langle\texttt{p}+\overline{Q},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Phase}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\psadd_{y^{\prime}_{0}=\texttt{su}}(\left\langle\texttt{p}(y^{\prime}_{0}),\texttt{s}(y^{\prime}_{0})\cdot\texttt{o}(y^{\prime}_{0})\right\rangle\psadd e^{2\pi i\overline{Q}(y^{\prime}_{0})}\left\langle\texttt{p}(y^{\prime}_{0}),\texttt{s}(y^{\prime}_{0})\cdot\texttt{o}(y^{\prime}_{0})\right\rangle)

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbb{C}\text{-vector space}}

\displaystyle\psadd_{y^{\prime}_{0}=\texttt{su}}(2|\operatorname{\texttt{cos}}\pi\overline{Q}(y^{\prime}_{0})|e^{2\pi i\frac{\overline{Q}}{2}})\left\langle\texttt{p}(y^{\prime}_{0}),\texttt{s}(y^{\prime}_{0})\cdot\texttt{o}(y^{\prime}_{0})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Phase,Scalar}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}+\frac{\overline{Q}}{2},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}HH}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}+\frac{y_{0}\cdot(y_{1}\oplus\overline{Q})}{2},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Phase-Bisector}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}+\frac{y_{1}+\oplus\overline{Q}}{4},2|\operatorname{\texttt{cos}}(\pi(y_{1}\oplus\overline{Q}))|\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}+\frac{y_{1}\oplus\overline{Q}}{4},2(1\oplus y_{1}\oplus\overline{Q})\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Change-Var}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}[y_{1}\leftarrow 1\oplus y_{1}\oplus\overline{Q}]+\frac{1\oplus y_{1}}{4},2y_{1}\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}[y_{1}\leftarrow 1\oplus y_{1}\oplus\overline{Q}]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}_{\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Filter}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\left\langle\texttt{p}[y_{1}\leftarrow\overline{Q}],2\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}[y_{1}\leftarrow\overline{Q}]\right\rangle

Finally, we target
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.582,0.0234,0.1523}Phase-equiv}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. At this point, we must loosen the
soundness to equality modulo
\tilde{}\mathcal{V}
instead of
\mathcal{V}
. Given
\texttt{p}_{2}
is
fully determined by the history since it shares no variables with the classical
memory stacks, we have,

\displaystyle\tilde{}\mathcal{V}(\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1}+\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}})=

\displaystyle\overline{\sum_{y,\text{history}(\texttt{h}[y])=\eta}\texttt{s}(y)\cdot e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}_{1}(y)+2\pi i\texttt{p}_{2}(\eta)}|\texttt{o}\rangle_{\text{Qu}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\sum_{y,\text{history}(\texttt{h}[y])=\eta}\texttt{s}(y)\cdot e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}_{1}(y)}|\texttt{o}\rangle_{\text{Qu}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{}\mathcal{V}(\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}})

Lemma 0 (Correctness of
\otimes
).

Let
h
and
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
be
HPS
satisfying the conditions for forming the
tensor product. Then,
\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime})(\eta\otimes\eta^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h})(\eta)\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}^{\prime})(\eta^{\prime})
for
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
histories on

\texttt{o}_{\text{Cl}}
and
\texttt{o}^{\prime}_{\text{Cl}}
respectively.

Proof.

Let
\texttt{h}_{1}=\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{s}_{1}\cdot\texttt{o}_{1}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{1}}
and
\texttt{h}_{2}=\left\langle\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}_{2}\cdot\texttt{o}_{2}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{2}}
be
HPS
satisfying the
conditions for forming the tensor product; that is,
\texttt{su}_{1}\cap\texttt{su}_{2}=\emptyset
and
\texttt{o}_{1}\cap\texttt{o}_{2}=\emptyset
, and let
\eta_{1}

be a history on
\texttt{o}_{{1}_{\text{Cl}}}
and
\eta_{2}
be a history on

\texttt{o}_{{2}_{\text{Cl}}}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\in\texttt{su}_{1},y_{2}\in\texttt{su}_{2}\\
\text{history}(\texttt{h}_{1}[y_{1}])=\eta_{1}\\
\text{history}(\texttt{h}_{2}[y_{2}])=\eta_{2}\end{array}}}\texttt{s}_{1}(y_{1})\texttt{s}_{2}(y_{2})e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}_{1}(y_{1})}e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}_{2}(y_{2})}|\texttt{o}_{1_{\text{Qu}}}\rangle\otimes|\texttt{o}_{2_{\text{Qu}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\phantom{\otimes}\sum_{y_{1}\in\texttt{su}_{1},\text{history}(\texttt{h}_{1}[y_{1}])=\eta_{1}}\texttt{s}_{1}(y_{1})e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}_{1}(y_{1})}|\texttt{o}_{1_{\text{Qu}}}\rangle

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\otimes\sum_{y_{2}\in\texttt{su}_{2},\text{history}(\texttt{h}_{2}[y_{2}])=\eta_{2}}\texttt{s}_{2}(y_{2})e^{2\pi i\texttt{p}_{2}(y_{2})}|\texttt{o}_{2_{\text{Qu}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{2})

∎

From Lemma
A.7
follows the soundness of the rewriting
rules for
\otimes
: associativity, commutativity, and bilinearity with respect
to
\psadd
:

•

Associativity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}((\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{2})\otimes\texttt{h}_{3})((\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2})\otimes\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{2})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2}\otimes\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{2}\otimes\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes(\texttt{h}_{2}\otimes\texttt{h}_{3}))(\eta_{1}\otimes(\eta_{2}\otimes\eta_{3}))

Noting that
\o_{1}\otimes(\o_{2}\otimes\o_{3})=(\o_{1}\otimes\o_{2})\otimes\o_{3}
.

•

Commutativity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{2})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2}\otimes\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{2}\otimes\eta_{1})

since the registers are indexed by addresses, so the order of the tensor
product does not matter, and
\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2}=\eta_{2}\otimes\eta_{1}
.

•

Bilinearity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}((\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2})\otimes\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{1})+\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{1}))\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1})(\eta_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{3})+\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2})(\eta_{1})\otimes\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{1}\otimes\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{3})+\mathcal{V}(\texttt{h}_{2}\otimes\texttt{h}_{3})(\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{3})

∎

Theorem A.8 (Monotonicity of
\otimes
, and
\llbracket P\rrbracket
with respect to

\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}
).

Let
\texttt{h}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}
,
P
be a program that exclusively uses registers in

\texttt{h}^{\prime}
, and
\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}
be an arbitrary
HPS
satisfying the conditions for
forming the tensor product with
h
. Then,

(1)

\texttt{h}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}
(also implying
\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime}
), and

(2)

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
\texttt{h}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}
, then, by definition,
\texttt{h}=\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}

with
|\texttt{h}_{a}|=1
and
\text{var}(\texttt{h}_{a})\cup X=\emptyset
.

(1)

Indeed,
\texttt{h}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}=(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a})\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes(\texttt{h}_{a}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime})
. Since
\otimes
is commutative (it is defined using
addresses for registers), we have
\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes(\texttt{h}_{a}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes(\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a})=(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
. Once again,

|\texttt{h}_{a}|=1
and
\text{var}(\texttt{h}_{a})\cup X=\emptyset
, so
(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}\Rrightarrow^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}
.

(3)

We prove that by induction on the structure of
P
with the induction
hypothesis:
\texttt{h}=\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}\implies\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
. We have the following cases:

•

If
P=\textbf{Skip}
:

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\texttt{h}=\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}=\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
.

•

If
P=\textbf{Measure}(q,c)
:
Both
q
and
c
are
registers of
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
. In
\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\texttt{h}[c\leftarrow\lceil q\rceil]=(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a})[c\leftarrow\lceil q\rceil]
, the change occurs in the
register
c
of
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
, so
\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\texttt{h}^{\prime}[c\leftarrow\lceil q\rceil]\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}=\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
.

•

If
P=c_{1}:=f(\lceil c_{2}\rceil)
:
In
HPS
, this
is identical to measurement as no hard distinction is made between
quantum and classical registers.

•

If
P=p;q
:
By induction,
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
. Since
\llbracket q\rrbracket
only acts on the
registers of
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
, it also only acts on the registers of

\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
, so that we have
\llbracket q\rrbracket(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}))=\llbracket q\rrbracket(\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime}))\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
. Thus,
\llbracket p;q\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\llbracket p;q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
.

•

If
P=\textbf{For }x^{I}_{n}=i,j\textbf{ do }p\textbf{ done}
:
This follows from the
sequencing case
P=p;q
and the unfolding of the for loop at
instantiation:
\llbracket P\rrbracket=\llbracket p[x_{n}^{I}:=i];\ldots;p[x_{n}^{I}:=j]\rrbracket
.

•

If
P=U(q)
:
Since
q
is a register of
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
, the
phase, norm, support, and changes in the values of the registers
q

are all contained in
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
and can be factored out the
HPS

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})
. More concretely,

–

(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a})[q\leftarrow b]=\texttt{h}^{\prime}[q\leftarrow b]\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
.

–

(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a})+_{p}\texttt{p}=(\texttt{h}^{\prime}+_{p}\texttt{p})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}

–

(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a})*_{n}\texttt{s}=(\texttt{h}^{\prime}*_{n}\texttt{s})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}

–

(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\otimes\texttt{h}_{a})\cup_{\texttt{su}}y=(\texttt{h}^{\prime}\cup_{\texttt{su}}y)\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
since the registers in

\texttt{h}_{a}
cannot be made to contain
y
with this operation
that only modifies registers in
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
.

•

If
P=\textbf{If }\bm{\texttt{b}}\textbf{ then }p\textbf{ else }q\textbf{ end}
:

By induction,
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
and

\llbracket q\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\llbracket q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
. Therefore,

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h})+\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket q\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}+\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
.
By linearity of
\otimes
, we then have

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=(\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})+\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime}))\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}

Since
b
can only concern registers in
\texttt{h}^{\prime}
, we do also have

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})=\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})+\bar{}\bm{\texttt{b}}\llbracket q\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})
, finally giving
\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h})=\llbracket P\rrbracket(\texttt{h}^{\prime})\otimes\texttt{h}_{a}
.

∎

Typing rules for the
\lambda-
calculus.

\begin{array}[]{lcll}L[R]&:=&\emptyset_{L[R]}\mid R\cdot L[R]&\mathit{LIST\ OF\ R\ SCHEME}\\
\hline\cr\operatorname{\texttt{creg}}&:=&\texttt{ident}^{C}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{creg}}[\texttt{i}:]\mid\operatorname{\texttt{creg}}[:\texttt{i}]\mid\operatorname{\texttt{creg}}[\texttt{i}_{1}:\texttt{i}_{2}]&Quantum\ registers\\
\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}&:=&\texttt{ident}^{Q}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}[\texttt{i}:]\mid\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}[:\texttt{i}]\mid\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}[\texttt{i}_{1}:\texttt{i}_{2}]&Classical\ registers\\
\texttt{b}_{1},\texttt{b}_{2}&:=&0\mid 1\mid y_{\texttt{i}}\mid\lceil\operatorname{\texttt{(c/q)reg}}[\texttt{i}]\rceil\mid\texttt{b}_{1}\wedge\texttt{b}_{2}\mid\texttt{b}_{1}\oplus\texttt{b}_{2}&\mathit{Booleans}\\
\texttt{i}_{1},\texttt{i}_{2}&:=&n\mid\sharp\operatorname{\texttt{(c/q)reg}}\mid\texttt{i}_{1}+_{i}\texttt{i}_{2}\mid\texttt{i}_{1}*\texttt{i}_{2}\mid\texttt{i}_{1}^{\texttt{i}_{2}}&\mathit{Integers}\\
\texttt{su}_{1},\texttt{su}_{2}&:=&\emptyset\mid\{y_{\texttt{i}}\}\mid\texttt{su}_{1}\cup\texttt{su}_{2}\mid\texttt{su}_{1}\setminus\texttt{su}_{2}&\textit{$\texttt{HPS}$ support}\\
\texttt{p},\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{p}_{2}&:=&0_{\texttt{p}}\mid\frac{\texttt{b}}{2^{\texttt{i}}}\mid\frac{\texttt{i}}{2^{\texttt{i}}}\mid\texttt{p}_{1}+_{\texttt{p}}\texttt{p}_{2}\mid\texttt{i}\cdot\texttt{p}\mid\texttt{b}\cdot\texttt{p}\mid-_{\texttt{p}}\texttt{p}&\mathit{Dyadics}\\
\texttt{a},\texttt{a}_{1},\texttt{a}_{2}&:=&2\pi\frac{\texttt{i}_{1}}{\texttt{i}_{2}}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{arccos}}\texttt{r}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{arcsin}}\texttt{r}\mid\texttt{i}*_{a}\texttt{a}\mid\texttt{b}*_{a}\texttt{a}\mid&\mathit{Angles}\\
&&\texttt{a}_{1}+_{a}\texttt{a}_{2}\mid-\texttt{a}\\
\texttt{r},\texttt{r}_{1},\texttt{r}_{2}&:=&\frac{\texttt{i}_{1}}{\texttt{i}_{2}}\mid\sqrt{\texttt{r}}\ \mid\operatorname{\texttt{cos}}\texttt{a}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{sin}}\texttt{a}\mid\texttt{r}_{1}+_{r}\texttt{r}_{2}\mid\texttt{r}_{1}*_{r}\texttt{r}_{2}\mid\frac{1}{\texttt{r}}&\mathit{Constructibles}\\
\operatorname{\texttt{qmem}}&:=&L[|\texttt{b}\rangle_{\operatorname{\texttt{qreg}}[\texttt{i}]}]&Quantum\ memory\\
\operatorname{\texttt{cmem}}&:=&L[{[\texttt{b}]}_{\operatorname{\texttt{creg}}[\texttt{i}]}]&Classical\ memory\\
\uparrow{\operatorname{\texttt{cmem}}}&:=&L[\operatorname{\texttt{cmem}}]&\operatorname{\texttt{cmem}}\mathit{\ stack}\\
\texttt{o}&:=&\operatorname{\texttt{qmem}}{}\cdot\uparrow{\operatorname{\texttt{cmem}}}&\mathit{Output}\\
\texttt{h}&:=&\left\langle\texttt{p},\texttt{s}\cdot\texttt{o}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}}&\texttt{HPS}\\
\end{array}

.
The grammar for
HPS
terms

(Add-L-distr)

(a+b)\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}a\texttt{h}\psadd b\texttt{h}

(Add-R-distr)

a(\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2})\equiv^{\texttt{p}}a\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd a\texttt{h}_{2}

(Add-comm)

\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}\psadd\texttt{h}_{1}

(Add-assoc)

\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd(\texttt{h}_{2}\psadd\texttt{h}_{3})\equiv^{\texttt{p}}(\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2})\psadd\texttt{h}_{3}

\texttt{h}_{2}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{3}

(Local-Add-rew)

\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{2}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{1}\psadd\texttt{h}_{3}

(Neutral-add)

\texttt{h}\psadd\left\langle x_{\texttt{p}},0\cdot x_{\texttt{o}}\right\rangle\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}

\texttt{su}_{1}\cap\texttt{su}_{2}=\emptyset

\texttt{Oad}_{1}\cap\texttt{Oad}_{2}=\emptyset

(Fact-Distr)

\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1},\texttt{s}_{1}\cdot\texttt{o}_{1}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{1}}\pstens\left\langle\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}_{2}\cdot\texttt{o}_{2}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{2}}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\left\langle\texttt{p}_{1}+\texttt{p}_{2},\texttt{s}_{1}*\texttt{s}_{2}\cdot\texttt{o}_{1}\cup\texttt{o}_{2}\right\rangle_{\texttt{su}_{1}\cup\texttt{su}_{2}}

(Fact-Distr) preconditions

(Fact-comm)

\texttt{h}_{1}\pstens\texttt{h}_{2}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{2}\pstens\texttt{h}_{1}

(Fact-Distr) preconditions

(Fact-assoc)

\texttt{h}_{1}\pstens(\texttt{h}_{2}\pstens\texttt{h}_{3})\equiv^{\texttt{p}}(\texttt{h}_{1}\pstens\texttt{h}_{2})\pstens\texttt{h}_{3}

\texttt{h}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}^{\prime\prime}

(Fact-Distr) preconditions

(Local-Fact-rew)

\texttt{h}_{1}\pstens\texttt{h}_{2}\equiv^{\texttt{p}}\texttt{h}_{1}\pstens\texttt{h}3
